Investing - Theory, News &amp; General • Re: Do you follow a Ferri/Bernstein/Swenson/other portfolio?

📅 Nov 14, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →



That's fascinating to me coming from you. So the whole concern over being able to harvest the foreign tax credits is overplayed in your opinion?
I would not use the word overplayed, because that assigns a subjective value. When I've done the math, the difference in total tax cost (VT vs similarly-weighted allocation of VTI + VXUS) is a single-digit number of basis points. For me, that's worth it for the convenience and behavioral finance benefit of not needing to rebalance. "I would have no qualms about it," as I said. Somebody else could decide that it's a big enough difference that they'd absolutely prefer the multiple-fund approach.
Interesting thanks. And since I started using a CPA a few years back, that savings might be even less. TBH, I don't know exactly how they decide what to charge me, but adding another form (to recover the foreign tax credit) probably doesn't make my tax return bill cheaper.

Investing - Theory, News &amp; General • Re: Why personal finance isn't taught, and, how to teach it to kids

📅 Nov 14, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

I think talking about money and normalizing discussing it is the first step. It shouldn't be this hush hush taboo topic. It is a part of everyone's everyday life and the earlier a child learns that the better. Start with simple things and move on to more complicated as they get older. I look forward to teaching my child about compound interest when she is mature enough to comprehend it. I have a few more years to wait.... For now I am happy to ask her how much something costs when she asks for it at the grocery store for example. Associating that there is a cost/desire for an item rather than just a desire is a simple start. Want that box of cheese its? How much does it cost etc. She knows I have "accounts" for her but she has no concept of what kind of accounts. She will know that she should always "save, invest, and spend" in that order. There is still plenty of time and my goal is that she is more financially literate than I ever was at her age. Not a major feat since I never even heard of an Roth Ira till my late 30's. I am very late to the game. She will be better prepared at a younger age.

I really enjoyed DividendDucks comment. I hope for similar stories of my own someday!

This Playful Florida Raised Ranch Is Proof That Bold Style Can Be Incredibly Timeless

📅 Nov 14, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Classic materials and a modern layout read as "traditional with a twist."

How do you turn a straightforward ranch-style home into something exciting and unexpected yet timeless? When a young family of four asked designer James Farmer to do just that for their central Florida abode, he opted for a gut renovation, using his homegrown knowledge of Southern style, years of interior expertise, and pointed enthusiasm to renovate ranches to create a highly functional and striking escape for his clients.

Located in an old, established neighborhood on a lake in Winter Park, Florida, the home was surrounded by mature live oaks and beautiful landscaping. “I would describe the area as old-school Florida with a touch of Mayberry,” says Farmer, referencing the fictional town from The Andy Griffith Show. The home itself was humble and honest—“no hiding its hand at the poker table,” says Farmer.

Alongside Skolfield Homes, Farmer kept the architectural style generally the same, with the most impactful changes being a complete overhaul of the galley kitchen and a relocation of the staircase from the side of the house to the middle, putting it in its deserved place as a focal point of the living room. The new kitchen is what he calls a “glorified galley,” with a sunny breakfast room off to the side that used to be a screened-in porch. He then reinforced the home as a "front door house," ensuring the formal entry space would actually be used rather than passed through by adding a bar perfect for fancy cocktail parties or a casual post-work martini.

For the actual interior, the designer layered various textures and bold patterns, combining them with hallmarks of grand Floridian style, like floral wallpapers, gold details, intricate trim, palm motifs, pecky cypress wallcoverings, and a coastal color palette. The line between indoor and outdoor living is further blurred with trellis details and a selection of timeworn brick and wood flooring. "We really leaned into this in a new way with this project because of its location," he says. "True, classic Florida can be so fun but also timeless, and that was our goal with this project."

Farmer encouraged his clients to splurge only on materials and pieces that'll truly stand the test of time, opting for classic finishes and prints that will age with the young family—an approach he details intimately in his newest book, Home Again: A Return to Gracious Interiors. Ultimately, the decadent interior paired with the modern structural changes naturally created what ended up being Farmer's motto for the home: "Traditional with a twist!"

FAST FACTSDesigner: James Farmer of James Farmer DesignsLocation: Winter Park, FloridaThe Space: A ranch-style home with 4 bedrooms and 3.5 bathrooms

Fabulous cocktail parties start in this formal space before migrating to other parts of the house. With no TV in the space, all eyes are drawn to the bar set behind a custom set of accent chairs by Thibaut, which is where Farmer's clients shake up (not stir) "the best martinis in Florida."

A color palette of peach, robin's egg blue, and butterscotch is celebrated in this room, the designer explains. By painting the ceiling in a blue tone—a trick commonly seen on Southern porches—he gave the room a subtle indoor-outdoor touch commonly seen on Southern porches.

Borrowed light from the kitchen and stairwell shines on the rich velvets and great patterns in this family room. “The color combo of terracotta and emerald with creamy white millwork creates a handsome and elegant palette,” Farmer adds.

Once a screened-in porch, Farmer kept the alfresco feel by covering the walls and ceiling with trelliage panels and outfitting the room with timeless white wicker furniture by Memoire Design (once known as Mainly Baskets Home). Vintage elephant garden stools now stand as whimsical side tables.

“We kept the original corner cabinets and wainscoting but gave it a bit of glam with a custom Gracie Studio wallpaper and lacquered ceiling,” Farmer says of the dining room, which kept its original footprint. The console tables are painted in a lemony yellow hue to match the window treatments and the room's other cheerful elements.

Farmer broke up this completely new, double galley kitchen into sections to make hosting a breeze. On one side of the counter is the seating and hangout zone, while the other side plays host to a functional working kitchen, outfitted with a Wolf range and Sub-Zero fridge. Think of it like traditional with a twist; "These clients are fabulous entertainers, and the island can also make a great buffet for parties," he says. "We like to think that The Golden Girls would thrive in this kitchen… Mid-century meets Maw-Maw!"

Old Chicago brick and palm motifs in the wallpaper make this breakfast hub feel more like a terrace than an interior enclave. Farmer created this room as part of the kitchen expansion—its bay window only enhances the porch-like vibe he wanted. "Sometimes geometry is your best friend," Farmer says, noting that the rug, table, and even shape of the room are octagonal. "You might not notice it, but it makes a moment."

“We were able to move the staircase in this house, which allowed for a more efficient floor plan for this young family,” he explains. “When you are under a major renovation, you can think outside of the box, or sometimes inside the box, just as easily.” Now the stairwell can live as a beautiful moment all its own at the center of the home, rather than pushed to the side.

The daughter was sleeping in a crib when Farmer first started working on his clients' house, so he wanted to design a room that could grow with her (by the time the project wrapped, she was in her big girl bed). The pink trellis wallpaper by Schumacher perfectly rides the line between feminine and elevated.

Touches of bamboo, palm, and pecky cypress introduce that old Florida feel, reminiscent of family vacations to the family's favorite spots in the Sunshine State, without being too on the nose.

“An interior design trick that is so old-school, but still so fun, is using a drapery fabric for wall covering and vice versa,” Farmer explains. “We were able to bring that from the primary bedroom to the primary bathroom.”

James Farmer is a Southern author, interior designer, and speaker known for his ability to create beautifully familiar and welcoming homes. To James, a home engages all of the senses—the pleasantries of colors and materials, the feel of the doorknob warmed by the sun, the smell of fresh laundry, the sound of the closing door, and the taste of supper on the table. Through his design firm, James works with clients to truly create a home.

Projects by James Farmer Designs can be found from the countryside of Connecticut to James's home state of Georgia. Though his work can be found throughout the country, James proudly has built his business in his hometown of Perry, Georgia.

‘Magical’ Tree House With 30-Foot Glass Living Room Returns to the Market

📅 Nov 14, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

A “magical” four-bedroom retreat encased in glass has returned to the market in Austin, TX, for $1,095,000.

The Glass Tree House debuted on the market for $1.5 million in August 2024 and has received several price cuts since then.

Custom slate stairs lead to a jaw-dropping living room with 30-foot-tall windows and custom rock walls carved into a cliffside, with tree-lined views that make you feel at one with nature.

“When you walk in, it feels like wherever you go, there is so much privacy and the view is very unique,” says listing agent Jenny Law of Kuper Sotheby’s International Realty. “It reminds me of a modern tree house that takes you far away from city life.”

Built in 2008, the 2,180-square-foot metal-clad contemporary was designed by architect John Allen to blur the boundaries between the indoor living spaces and the outdoor setting.

“The sellers use it as a short-term rental property,” Law notes. “It’s a nice retreat and would be a popular second home for someone because of its views and location, or it could be used as a primary residence for its school system.”

The sleek kitchen has custom black cabinetry, honey onyx countertops, built-in seating, and stainless-steel appliances.

The glass-surrounded primary suite has a soaking tub, a moss rock shower, Calacatta marble countertops, marble flooring, and access to a private terrace.

“It’s located in Apache Shores, where there are not a lot of restrictions in that area,” Law says. “It’s about 15 to 20 minutes from downtown Austin, but you feel like you are out in nature with these nice hill country views.”

A second lot, which could allow for a future pool, workshop, or guesthouse with applicable approvals, is available for separate purchase.

The property is also minutes from Lake Travis and Lake Austin, a residents-only boat launch, an 18-hole disc golf course, a soccer field, a community hall, a swimming pool, and sports courts.

“In Austin, there are so many homes that look the same, but this one is unique and beautiful,” Law says. “You truly feel like you are living outside.”

Personal Finance (Not Investing) • Re: Switching Medicare medigap insurers (both Plan G)

📅 Nov 14, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

We have been on Medicare with a supplemental/medigap plan for a little over 2 years. Seems we could save maybe $100/month next year by switching to a different medigap insurer. Has anyone done this? The coverage is equivalent (they are both Plan G) but I am concerned that the HIPAA release form we are to sign seems overly far reaching. It states that in addition to all medical history they may obtain Social Security information, retirement income, financial information, earnings and employment history.

We certainly don’t remember this level of disclosure at the initial (age 65) signup when coverage can not be denied.

It could be that my current insurer was the lowest cost when we lived in state A but now we have moved to state B. Additionally, we of course don’t know which will be the lower cost provider far into the future.

So I ask, if it were you, would you consider this move or stay with your original choice?

Appreciate any and all help!
I thought that moving to a different state allows a switch in insurers without underwriting; I am in NY so can switch anytime without underwriting so haven't kept up with these details

James Gunn Says ‘No One From Skydance’ Has Seen ‘Superman’ Sequel ‘Man of Tomorrow’ as Rumors Fly After His DC Studios Exit

📅 Nov 14, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

James Gunn took to Threads to clarify a rumor about his exit from DC Studios: It had nothing to do with the upcoming “Superman” sequel “Man of Tomorrow” worrying executives at Warner Bros.’ new parent company, Skydance. Rumors have circulated on social media alleging the film’s poor quality is one of the reasons Gunn got the boot after four years as co-CEO of DC Studios alongside Peter Safran. The rumors snowballed into claims Skydance would dump “Man of Tomorrow” onto streaming via HBO Max amid yet another DC Universe overhaul, this time under president and chief creative officer Jeremy Latcham. Related Stories Shin Min-a, Ju Ji-hoon, Lee Jong-suk and Lee Se-young Take Webtoon Juggernaut 'The Remarried Empress' to Disney+ and Hulu Disney+ and ‘The House of Flowers’ Director Manolo Caro Prep Series Adaptation of ‘Blood Wedding’ by Federico García Lorca (EXCLUSIVE)

“There are a lot of untrue rumors going around, but one of them is that Skydance has seen ‘Man of Tomorrow’ and that all sorts of sweeping changes are being made,” Gunn posted on his Threads account denying the rumors. “No one from Skydance has even seen the movie – the only people that have are Peter Safran and a very small trusted group of friends and family. So any stories around this narrative are false.”

Gunn and Safran’s exits from DC Studios were announced Nov. 9. Their departures came as a shock considering the duo was named as part of Skydance’s business leadership team when Paramount’s $111 billion acquisition of Warner Bros. Discovery closed in October. Gunn and Safran became the heads of DC Studios in 2022 and their four-year stint included film releases like “Superman,” “Supergirl” and “Clayface” and television series like “Creature Commandos” and “Lanterns,” among others.

Soon after the news broke, Gunn took to social media to express his feelings about the job and revealed that he had grown weary by the executive job.

“What only the closest people to me have known over the past four years is my time at DC Studios has been trying, to say the least,” Gunn wrote. “I dislike the at-times corporate nature of the job (thanks to Peter for taking the brunt of that). I’m always a very hard worker, but as a filmmaker I would at least get to take short breaks to unwind, and I’ve stolen even that time from both myself and my loved ones over the past four years”

In his statement, Gunn also revealed that he’s “engaged on another DC project which I’m writing” in addition to post-production on “Man of Tomorrow.” The “Superman” sequel is currently on the release calendar for July 9, 2027.

The 5 Healthiest Fast Food Salads, According To Nutritionists

📅 Nov 14, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

A drive-thru doesn’t have to derail your diet.

Fast food chains aren’t exactly famous for their salads. They usually fall into two categories: a sad bowl of lettuce added to menus as an afterthought, or a vehicle for fried, calorie-dense ingredients that offset any nutritional upsides. But some chains do make an effort to sell salads worth ordering.

To identify the best better-for-you fast-food salads, we asked three nutrition experts to weigh in. Here are the menu items they recommend, along with some tips and tricks for maximizing their nutrition.

First, we need to debunk salad’s diet-friendly reputation. “A salad isn't automatically the healthiest choice on a fast food menu,” says Kathy Levin, RDN, CDCES, dipACLM, LDN. “Once you add fried chicken, bacon, cheese, candied nuts, crunchy toppings, and a creamy dressing, some salads land in the same calorie and fat range as a burger and fries.”

Taking all of those components away isn’t always a solution either. A sad salad with just lettuce and a smattering of vegetables is more like rabbit food than a satisfying meal for humans. According to Briana V. Reamer, MS, RDN, LDN, the secret is getting enough fiber, which she says “will keep you satisfied and full until your next meal.” Take advantage of the restaurant's add-ons—like beans, avocado, and more vegetables—to make your salad more substantial.

Experts also recommend watching your saturated fat intake, specifically with your protein and dressing. Grilled chicken generally has fewer calories and less saturated fat than deep-fried alternatives. Consider your dressing options, too. “If you’re not sure what to pick, look for a vinaigrette or olive-oil-based dressing,” says Tina Sergi, MS, RD, CSSD, CSCS, TSAC-F.

And, as a rule of thumb, order your dressing on the side. It keeps your salad from getting soggy on the drive home, for one, but it also gives you more portion control. “Using only part of the packet cuts saturated fat and sodium, especially when they're already on the higher end,” Sergi adds.

Of course, the dressing is only part of the equation. With those tips in mind, here are the five salads that made the cut.

This regional chain is known for its fire-grilled chicken, and this salad packs plenty of it. Each portion contains a double serving of citrus-marinated chicken breast, sliced avocado, cotija cheese, salsa fresca, and tortilla strips over a bed of greens. It's served with a creamy Mexican-inspired Caesar dressing.

El Pollo Loco strikes the right balance between substantial and light with this dish. Before you add dressing, the entire salad contains 420 calories and 52 grams of protein. “The fire-grilled chicken makes it very filling, the avocado adds heart-healthy fat and fiber, and the salsa fresca adds flavor without much sodium or sugar,” Levin says.

Like with most salads, the dressing significantly raises the calorie count—in this case, it adds 440 calories if you use the entire serving. If you're closely monitoring your daily intake, Levin advises going easy on the dressing.

Culver’s may be best known for its ButterBurgers, but experts say you shouldn’t sleep on its salads. The Chicken Cashew Salad in particular is a great choice. It starts with a bed of mixed greens, topped with cheddar, grape tomatoes, cucumbers, and roasted cashews. You can also choose from several dressings to finish it off.

With grilled chicken, the salad comes in at 430 calories and 41 grams of protein before adding dressing. Culver’s also offers six dressing varieties ranging from 50 to 260 calories.

“This is a great fiber- and protein-packed option,” says Sergi. “Plus, the cashews add healthy fats, copper to support energy production, and magnesium for optimal nerve and muscle function.”

This Lone Star staple sells a Cobb salad that’s a lighter alternative to its lineup of burgers and sandwiches. It comes loaded with all of the essentials: greens, bacon, a hard-boiled egg, grape tomatoes, carrots, and shredded cheese. You can also choose from three chicken options (the grilled chicken filet is, naturally, lighter than the fried options).

Without dressing, Whataburger's Cobb with grilled chicken has 430 calories and 44 grams of protein. It's also a source of fiber and is low in sugar, according to Reamer. “Salads high in fiber help with weight management and satisfy hunger.”

Some of Whataburger's creamier dressings add 200 to 280 calories, while the balsamic vinaigrette has 180.

For a fried chicken chain, Chick-fil-A makes some surprisingly stellar salads. The Market Salad starts with mixed greens and is topped with strawberries, blueberries, red and green apples, blue cheese, roasted almonds, and the chain’s Harvest Nut Granola. Chick-fil-A also has the most protein options on this list. The grilled chicken can come in nugget or filet form, warm or cold, and spicy or regular. Talk about variety!

The different grilled chicken options provide between 26 and 32 grams of protein, according to Chick-fil-A's nutrition guide. Levin also says you can forgo chicken entirely for a meatless meal that doesn't feel like an afterthought. “Berries and apples bring fiber, antioxidants and natural sweetness, which makes it one of the most plant-forward options on the list.”

The Market Salad is typically paired with Chick-fil-A's Zesty Apple Cider Vinaigrette Dressing, which contains 230 calories per packet. But the chain is known for its sauces, after all, so you can swap it out for another option. There's a Light Balsamic Vinaigrette with 80 calories, Fat-Free Honey Mustard with 90, and Light Italian Dressing with just 25.

Zaxby's is the third chicken chain on this list, and it stands out by offering a salad with Asian-inspired flavors. The Asian Zensation Zalad (not Salad) adds shredded carrots, cabbage, wonton strips, and Asian Slaw to mixed greens, along with a drizzle of teriyaki sauce. It also comes with a vegetable egg roll on the side.

This salad is loaded with plant-based toppings, which is why Levin also recommends the garden version as a vegetarian option. Zaxby's lists that version at 390 calories, while the grilled chicken version comes in at 530 calories and 39 grams of protein, before adding dressing.

Alongside the teriyaki drizzle, Zaxby's offers Citrus Vinaigrette with the salad, which has 110 calories per packet. It's one of the chain's lighter options. But if you're watching your calorie intake, you can swap in Lite Vinaigrette, which contains just 30 calories.

Why Bruce Buffer Traded the Octagon for the Steakhouse in New Bud Light Campaign

📅 Nov 14, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The UFC ring announcer stars in a new Bud Light spot, as the beer brand and Omaha Steaks launch a new fan engagement play.

As the UFC returns to Madison Square Garden Saturday night, UFC ring announcer Bruce Buffer is trading the octagon for a steakhouse in a new Bud Light spot.

The campaign sees Buffer swap places with a steakhouse server so they can watch the big UFC fight, leaving him to serve the customers.

“I love when I perform or even when I’m doing commercials to be self-deprecating,” Buffer tells The Hollywood Reporter about the spot. “I like making fun of myself and just having fun for comedy purposes, and the creative people at Bud Light, you know, when they send me the storyboards, I go, ‘This is going to be awesome.’ So I totally dug my teeth into it. I loved it.” Related Stories Movies Ben Stiller Gives Update on His New York Knicks Documentary: "The Pressure Is on" Business Why Jordan Brand Is Using a 'Space Jam' Docuseries to Launch "Jordan TV"

The Bud Light spot was released ahead of UFC 334, which will take place tonight at Madison Square Garden in New York City. Bud Light is an official sponsor of UFC, with its spots typically running during the broadcast both for viewers and those in the arena.

“Bud Light’s creative team is just really fantastic at what they do, and looking at that, I trust them to come up with what they came up with, and they also give me the freedom to give feedback,” says Buffer. “It’s really, really cool, and it’s great when you work with people that are that comfortable to work with and respect the fact that they’re not damaging your brand, but yet enhancing it.”

In addition to the spot, Bud Light is partnering with UFC and Omaha Steaks on a “Fight Night Feast” package, which will bundle tomahawk steaks, Bud Light beer money, a Paramount+ gift card and UFC training gloves for $99.

“We know the best part of fight night is getting together with friends over an ice-cold Bud Light and some great food,” said Todd Allen, senior VP of marketing for Bud Light at Anheuser-Busch. “And with UFC 335 set to close out the year, we wanted to create something for UFC fans that makes fight night easy to enjoy. Fight Night Feast delivers everything needed for an unforgettable night of UFC action directly to fans’ doorsteps.”

But it’s also a testament to how far UFC has come. What was once an outcast sport now counts America’s biggest beer brand as a sponsor.

“When I started with the UFC 30 years ago, we were a spectacle. We weren’t what I would consider a sport,” Buffer recalls. “It was like a blood sport, like the movie. It was what style is the best? It grew with the rules and became mixed martial arts seven or eight years into its evolutionary process, to now where it’s a mainstream sport, to where we’re accepted by the Paramount networks and all the other big networks of this world, and to get to the point now where we’re working with Bud Light and mainstream sponsors that work with the other major sports in the world, it shows that UFC is truly the biggest fighting event in the world and is a mainstream sport. It’s like we made it. It’s a clarification that we’re there.”

Tuttosport: Centre-back, right-back and striker – Krösche faces big task in January

📅 Nov 14, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Markus Krösche will be announced as Milan’s new Head of Football in January, according to Tuttosport, and an important task awaits him.

After a summer during which Gerry Cardinale personally worked alongside an internal group on the mercato business, he’s expected to get some help soon. Indeed, an agreement has been reached with Eintracht Frankfurt for Krösche, who is expected to be announced at the start of January.

Read SempreMilan ad-free and get access to exclusive news. Click here for a free trial!

In other words, the new Head of Football will be thrown straight into the fire as Milan are expected to make some reinforcements during the winter mercato. The start of the season has indeed uncovered some depth issues which need to be fixed.

According to today’s edition of Tuttosport, as transcribed by MilanNews, Milan are looking to make three signings in January: a centre-back, a right-back and a striker. This means that Krösche is in for quite the task, even if there is a budget to support him.

For the centre-back position, Goncalo Inacio remains the big target but the Rossoneri also have some other names in mind. On the right flank, the early return of Zachary Athekame from Lyon still cannot be ruled out, though there are other options here too.

Up front, Nicolo Tresoldi appears to be the favourite but the price tag could make things difficult, especially since we all know how stern Club Brugge are in negotiations. In any case, once the Krösche announcement arrives, we can expect to at least see some movement.

Warren Buffett Said This 1 Mistake Cost Berkshire About $10 Billion. Here’s How Investors Can Avoid It.

📅 Nov 14, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Buffett said that hesitating over a small price gap on Walmart cost Berkshire about $10 billion. Here’s how to avoid the same trap.

Warren Buffett never hid from his investment mistakes. Instead, he wanted to learn from them. One of his most expensive blunders wasn't a bad purchase, but one he never completed.

At Berkshire Hathaway's (BRKA +0.47%)(BRKB +0.53%) 2004 annual meeting, Buffett confessed that his worst mistake was Walmart (WMT +1.51%). While he initially wanted to buy 100 million shares of the retail giant, Berkshire bought only a small amount because, as the price went up, Buffett waited for it to come back down, which never happened. His reluctance to pay a little more turned out to be a $10 billion mistake.

The mistake wasn't buying a bad stock. It was getting too anchored to a price on a good one. That's a trap almost every investor falls into, and it's easy to avoid once you know what to look for.

Walmart was exactly the kind of business Buffett wanted to own. It was a dominant low-cost leader. He set his sights on buying 100 million shares at a pre-split price of $23. Buffett bought some shares, but then the price rose slightly. He thought it might come back down, so he waited. That pullback never came. In hindsight, he said paying a little more would have been inconsequential compared to the gains the stock would go on to deliver.

Buffett did try to fix this mistake. Berkshire built a new Walmart stake starting in 2005, which was worth nearly $6 billion by 2016, the year it sold most of it.

Berkshire's missed gains on Walmart show why anchoring can be so costly. If you think a $50 stock has the potential to gain 50% over the next few years, paying 5% more ($52.50) than your desired price would yield a 43% return. That's not a bad return. Waiting for the price to come back down to your target, on the other hand, risks the entire upside if it never does. A small premium costs you a little in the long run, while a missed position costs you the whole gain.

The lesson here isn't to pay any price for a stock. Buffett's Walmart mistake served as a valuable lesson because Walmart's an exceptional business, so paying a few percentage-point premium was small relative to its upside. However, chasing a stock that has already run up, or paying a premium for an average business, are different mistakes. It's fine to pay a little bit more than you'd like for a high-quality business, but don't go overboard.

Patience and price discipline are good habits, but Buffett's Walmart miss shows how they can backfire when a small price gap keeps you out of a great business. Decide what a stock is worth before you buy, set your purchases in advance, and weigh any premium against the upside you expect. That way, your discipline protects you from overpaying without costing you the opportunity.

This Tractor Supply Mini-Excavator May Not Be The Smallest, But It Is One Of The Cutest

📅 Nov 14, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

We may receive a commission on purchases made from links.

Remember playing with your Tonka trucks as a wee kiddo? If you grew up before tablets displaced little metal keepsakes, you ruled an imaginary work site, moving clumps of sand, dirt, and mud from one place to other spots. Granted, those other places were often your hair, shirt, and pants pockets. Oh, those were the days. Fret not, forever child — Yardmax has a tool that bridges the gap between practical equipment and a rideable Tonka-style toy.

It's the Yardmax YE0610, a 1,432-pound mini-excavator with a host of available attachments, from a claw to a grading bucket and rake.  Better yet, you might just be able to scoot on down to your local Tractor Supply and pick one up.

That said, Yardmax's diminutive earth mover isn't the smallest thing in the mini-excavator market. In fact, if you want to get crazy with your tiny tools, Honda and Komatsu once teamed up to make what may very well be the cutest little digging machine ever.

You've seen the big stuff. You know, the massive, earth-moving heavy construction equipment required to build a modern highway. Monstrous bulldozers, cold milling machines, and long-necked dinosaur-ish excavators are all part of the process. Some of these dirt-munching excavators can get properly huge, with the biggest examples tipping the scales at over 2 million pounds. Yes, you read that right.

Other excavators, however, go for more of a park-it-in-your-garage footprint. That's what the Yardmax YE0610 mini-excavator is all about. At just 27.5 inches wide, the wee digging machine's width takes up considerably less floor space than a common go-kart. Even with the canopy installed on the YE0610-575003 model, the mini-excavator is 62.4 inches tall and 1,674 pounds. Opt for the topless base YE0610, and that weight drops to 1,432 pounds. Not exactly a 2-million-pound earth mover.

Okay, so it's cute. Can it dig? Yardmax asserts the YE0610 can dig up to 47.2 inches deep, or just under four feet. Then there are the attachments. Depending on the model, the Yardmax can use a claw, grading bucket, or rake for your digging, filling, clearing, and leveling tasks. As far as power goes, the mini-excavator features a 306-cc engine capable of hustling the little machine at a cautious 1-mph pace. As expected, the excavator can rotate 360 degrees, regardless of track position.

This wee workhorse might be one of the wildest things Tractor Supply sells. Then again, it might not be. After all, this is a retailer that also offers a teeny tiny minibike to awaken your inner child.

As of this writing, the Yardmax YE0610, complete with its removable canopy, grading bucket, and rake, is for sale at Tractor Supply for $6,999.99. Want to save a couple hundred bucks on your backyard digging machine? The same setup is available at Walmart for $6,799. Of course, if you'd rather go in another direction entirely, Tractor Supply also sells a Power Wheels-esque mini truck. Your mini truck won't likely do any excavating for you, though.

Here's the thing, though: the Yardmax YE0610 isn't the smallest thing on the mini-excavator market. Croba, a sub-brand of MechMaxx, produces a MEC06 mini-excavator that weighs 87 pounds less than the Yardmax and undercuts the Tractor Supply price for the YE0610 by nearly $2,000, though its max digging depth falls shy of the YE0610 by 3.9 inches.

Like a shrinking set of nesting dolls, things can get even smaller. Komatsu, a Japanese heavy machinery manufacturer, joined forces with Honda in 2022 to produce an unbearably cute rideable excavator for the Japanese market. The result was the Komatsu PC01E-1, a 750-pound electric micro excavator with unmistakable toy aisle looks.

Better yet, the PC01E-1 falls just shy of the 771.6-pound max load capacity of Japanese mini trucks. So, go ahead, load your pint-sized electric excavator into your tiny truck.

Why Airlines Schedule Flights To Take Longer Than They Used To—Even Though It Costs Them More

📅 Nov 14, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Former Ohio Attorney General Dave Yost says his flight to Washington’s National Airport still takes about 50 minutes, but the published schedule has grown to an hour and 42 minutes. He thinks he’s figured out why: airlines get to look more on-time by giving themselves longer to arrive. He’s right that the schedule determines whether a flight counts as on time. But airlines pay for those extra minutes, and they generally have good reasons to avoid adding more than they need. My flight to DCA takes about 50 minutes. It used to be listed as an hour & change. The *posted* flight duration keeps getting longer, now 1hr 42 min. Still takes 50 minutes. I can only conclude that these posted durations are used in determining on-time statistics. — Dave Yost (@DaveYostOH) November 12, 2026 The Schedule Includes Time On The Ground Airlines publish block time: the time scheduled from leaving the departure gate to reaching the arrival gate. That includes taxiing, waiting for a runway, flying, and getting to a gate at the other end. And National airport is a particularly odd place to dismiss congestion. In a January update, the Federal Aviation Administration explained that it had reduced the airport’s hourly arrival rate from 36 to 26 after the January 2025 collision, before increasing it to 30. The airport could handle more – if the air traffic control system hadn’t had such challenges. More demand concentrated into busy periods, limited runway and gate capacity, controller shortages and equipment problems all affect how long the trip takes. The problem also isn’t just the average. It’s how much travel time varies. An airline needs a schedule that works when there’s a normal amount of congestion, not just when the plane gets straight to the runway and encounters no delay at the other end. If that variability grows, the schedule can need more time even when the flight takes 50 minutes in the air. I made this point when covering claims about increased schedule padding in 2023. Airport and air traffic control bottlenecks make trip time less predictable. Airlines build that uncertainty into their schedules. Airlines Pay For Those Extra Minutes I’ve been pushing back on the simple ‘they’re juking the stats’ explanation since at least 2015. Making flights look longer is an expensive way to improve a scorecard. Crew costs. Scheduled time can determine pay even when a flight arrives early. American Airlines flight attendants, for instance, receive flight time pay based on the greater of scheduled or actual block time for each flight. Less productive airplanes. Add time across the day’s flying and there’s less room for another revenue flight. The airline can wind up needing more airplanes and crews to operate the same service. Less attractive itineraries. Longer trips can lose bookings to faster connections, competing airlines, or driving. Airlines want to sell you a convenient schedule. Back in 2015, I cited Southwest Airlines’ estimate that adding just a couple of minutes of block time across its schedule would cost the equivalent of 8 to 10 airplanes’ daily flying. You can’t just push flights later in the day, because airlines need to schedule flights when people want to fly. And airlines help create some of their own congestion through banked schedules at hubs. Packing flights into connecting waves makes more itineraries possible, but it also puts more demand on gates, runways and employees at the same time. Longer Schedules Can Improve More Than The Statistics The government counts an arrival as on time when the plane reaches the gate less than 15 minutes after its scheduled arrival. But if an airline keeps missing its arrival times by small margins, increasing block time can be a sensible first step toward fixing reliability. It’s acknowledging how long operating the flight actually takes, including the delays that occur often enough to plan for. American announced longer block times alongside a redesign of their Dallas operation, moving from 9 large connecting waves to 13 smaller ones in April. I explained why that investment could pay for itself through fewer missed connections, delayed bags and disrupted aircraft and crews. In April, American’s Dallas hub chief reported that missed connections were down 50%. At United Airlines’ 2019 media day, an executive explained that 25% of their flights were expected to arrive 10 minutes early. That gave them room to hold some departures for connecting passengers and still arrive on time through their ConnectionSaver program. United had started improving its reliability with longer block times. Arriving Early Can Create A Gate Problem Of course, extra time in the schedule doesn’t create extra gates. Get in early and the gate assigned to your flight may still have another airplane on it. An empty gate may belong to another airline, be unsuitable for your aircraft, or lack the employees needed to bring you in and operate the jetbridge. I covered Doug Parker explaining this problem in 2018: American used essentially all its hub gate capacity at peak times, and a few airplanes failing to leave could leave arriving flights with nowhere to go. So you can land early, sit waiting, and reach the terminal around the originally scheduled time. The government’s arrival clock keeps running until gate arrival. Regardless, it’s not all a conspiracy to juke the stats. Often it’s just a realistic expectation for how long the airline thinks on average it’ll take to travel, though sometimes they beat those expectations. The real solution isn’t just tightening schedules, it’s improving throughput – more capacity in air travel control and at airports. How Much Does An Airbus A380 Engine Cost?

📅 Nov 14, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Ask what an Airbus A380 engine costs, and the most honest answer is that the sticker price barely tells the story. Neither Rolls-Royce nor Engine Alliance publishes a catalog figure for the Trent 900 or the GP7200, and the biggest numbers attached to them in public are billion-dollar contracts that cover much more than the engines themselves. According to analyst estimates reported by AviationPros, a single GP7200 once carried a list price of roughly $7 million to $10.5 million. Yet one engine's share of Emirates' 2015 Rolls-Royce deal works out several times higher, and that difference points to where the real cost lies.

This article traces that difference back to the published estimates for the contracts airlines actually signed. Two engine families sit at the center of the story. Rolls-Royce builds the three-shaft Trent 900, while Engine Alliance, a 50-50 venture between GE Aerospace and Pratt & Whitney, builds the GP7200. Both powered the A380-800, the only member of the A380 family to reach service, but neither was ever simply sold as a piece of hardware.

There is no published list price, so every answer is necessarily an estimate. The analyst range quoted above came from an older report written while the A380 was still being delayed, and Pratt & Whitney declined to confirm it, noting that airlines usually receive discounts on large orders. After two decades of inflation and engine upgrades, it likely understates today's replacement cost. More importantly, it describes a bare engine, which is rarely what an airline actually signs for, and says nothing about spare engines, tooling, or the support network required to keep an engine flying.

The contracts signed by major A380 operators show why the simple number can be misleading. According to Emirates' own announcement, its April 2015 deal with Rolls-Royce for Trent 900 engines and a long-term TotalCare package covering 50 A380s was worth $9.2 billion, although a footnote put the order-book value at $6.1 billion. That figure included the engines and long-term support rather than representing a simple purchase price. Etihad's GP7200 deal was structured similarly: GE Aerospace said in its original announcement that the airline selected GP7200s for ten A380s and signed a multiyear Fleet Management Agreement covering maintenance, repair and overhaul, with a total value of more than $1.3 billion. A subsequent GE Aerospace announcement put the value at up to $1.5 billion. Rolls-Royce described the Emirates order as the largest in its history in its press release.

These figures therefore cannot be treated as straightforward engine prices. Emirates' $9.2 billion package covered 200 Trent 900 engines plus TotalCare, while Etihad's $1.3 billion-plus GP7200 agreement covered engines for ten A380s together with long-term fleet management and maintenance. Dividing the headline contract values by the number of installed engines would produce roughly $46 million per engine for the Emirates package and more than $32.5 million per engine for Etihad's original $1.3 billion figure, but those calculations include services and should not be mistaken for the price of the hardware itself.

The reason these numbers diverge is straightforward: an A380 engine contract is rarely only an engine purchase. The Emirates and Etihad packages bundled hardware with years of servicing. TotalCare covers overhauls, spare parts, and health monitoring, while Etihad's agreement included long-term fleet management. Engine makers can heavily discount the hardware and recover part of their investment through the aftermarket, a model established in the commercial engine business for decades.

The size of those discounts can make a published list price almost theoretical. As AirInsight reported from Rolls-Royce's patent lawsuit against Pratt & Whitney, Rolls-Royce said it had to discount the Trent 900 by more than 87%, compared with a normal discount of 77%. That makes it difficult to treat the nominal price of an engine as a reliable measure of what an airline actually pays.

The service contract is a crucial part of the economics. Aircraft Commerce explains that most Rolls-Royce engines are maintained under TotalCare packages, with services selected from a menu and billed per engine flight hour, giving airlines a predictable cost. AirInsight also wrote that Engine Alliance said it offered its engine at cost in the Emirates competition, only to be undercut by Rolls-Royce. For an A380 operator, the real purchase is consequently a package of hardware, spares and years of support rather than four isolated engines.

Simple Flying makes the same broader point about A380 ownership in its comparison of A380 and 747 costs: the purchase price is only the first part of the aircraft's financial story. For the engines, that distinction becomes even more important once they begin accumulating flight hours.

A closer look at the combination of GEnx strengths and Trent 1000 weaknesses that have led many airlines to opt for the GEnx.

The maintenance figures show just how quickly the original purchase price becomes only one part of the equation. According to Aircraft Commerce's owner's and operator's guide to the Trent family, published in 2012, a Trent 900 first shop visit typically cost $5.0 million to $5.3 million, while a second visit cost $6.2 million to $6.5 million. The first removals were expected after 23,000 to 28,000 engine flight hours.

The same guide put the Trent 900's life-limited parts at $4.9 million for disks and shafts plus another $1.9 million for fan blades and related parts, or about $6.8 million altogether. That is roughly the same order of magnitude as the old analyst estimate for an entire GP7200. These components also have defined service lives. The Trent 900's 18 disk and shaft items were certified for anywhere from 1,000 to 12,500 flight cycles against a 15,000-cycle target, meaning early-build engines could face heavier and earlier work.

Aircraft Commerce translated those maintenance costs into reserves of $245 to $291 per engine flight hour through the first shop visit, rising to $320 to $407 through the second. Across all four engines, that means an A380 could carry roughly $980 to $1,160 in engine reserves per flight hour at the first interval alone, in 2012 dollars. Because those figures are now more than a decade old, they should be treated as a historical floor rather than a current forecast. Newer estimates run higher: aircraft broker Jettly puts full Trent 900 overhauls at $10 million to $15 million each.

The maintenance cycle also explains why the support business remains important even though A380 production has ended. As Aviation Week's data tool forecasts, the Trent 900 fleet will see 548 overhaul shop visits over the next decade. Those visits involve much more than simply removing and reinstalling an engine, and the engineering behind the bill helps explain why the original hardware is so expensive.

The scale of the A380's powerplants is the first clue to their complexity. Each engine swings a 116-inch (296-cm) fan and produces enormous thrust. Aircraft Commerce gives the Trent 900 a maximum thrust rating of up to 84,000 lbf (374 kN), while Engine Alliance lists the GP7200 at 70,000 to 81,500 lbf (311 to 363 kN). The GP7200 also has an 8.8 bypass ratio and a pressure ratio of more than 36, while its 116-inch fan uses 24 swept, hollow titanium blades.

The GP7200 is also a genuinely multinational engine rather than a simple GE-Pratt & Whitney combination. Engine Alliance's current component breakdown assigns the fan and accessory gearbox to Pratt & Whitney, while Safran supplies the low-pressure compressor and booster and the high-pressure compressor. GE Aviation is responsible for the combustor, high-pressure turbine, and FADEC, while MTU Aero Engines supplies the low-pressure turbine. The arrangement combines technologies and manufacturing expertise from several major engine companies in a single powerplant.

The Trent 900 follows a different route with its three-shaft architecture. Aircraft Commerce notes that hollow, wide-chord fan blades reduce weight and drag across the Trent family. It describes the Trent 900 as a scaled-down three-shaft design with 24 swept fan blades, a tiled combustor that cools its inner walls with a film of air so it can burn hotter and cleaner, and a contra-rotating turbine that allows smaller guide vanes to perform the same work. Each feature contributes to fuel burn, noise, or performance, but each also requires precise manufacturing and inspection.

Rolls-Royce said in its 2015 announcement of the Emirates order that the Trent 900 had powered the first commercial A380 in 2007 and was already in service with eight operators across more than 70 aircraft, having accumulated over four million flight hours. The same Rolls-Royce announcement, reproduced by AviationPros. This was therefore a mature engine program by the time Emirates selected it for another 50 A380s.

All that engineering had to be scaled for an exceptionally large aircraft. According to Simple Flying's look back at 20 years of the A380, the A380-800 weighs up to 1,234,600 lb (560,000 kg) at takeoff and spans 261 feet, 8 inches (79.75 meters). That scale makes the engines technically impressive, but technical efficiency alone could not solve the A380's larger economic problem.

Its connection to the Airbus A380 reveals how planemaker reshaped its flight decks.

The Trent 900 and GP7200 were designed to make a four-engine aircraft as efficient as possible, and they achieved meaningful gains. Aircraft Commerce's 2012 comparison of Trent-powered aircraft found that an A380 with Trent 970 engines burned 35,319 US gallons (133,700 liters) of block fuel on a Frankfurt-New York flight. That equated to 0.0196 US gallons per seat-mile (0.046 liters per seat-km), the best result among the four-engine aircraft examined, although it remained slightly worse than the twin-engine A330s and 777-200ERs on unrestricted routes. The analysis assumed 486 seats on the A380.

Engine Alliance presents the GP7200's economics in more favorable terms. The manufacturer says the engine can deliver up to $500,000 in annual fuel savings per aircraft and a payload-range advantage worth up to $5.5 million a year. In a 2010 press release, the company also said Airbus credited the engine with a 0.5% fuel-consumption improvement and that dispatch reliability stood at 99.9% after nearly two years in service. These are manufacturer claims rather than independent cost calculations, but they show what Engine Alliance considered the engine's economic selling points when airlines were choosing between the two A380 powerplants.

The numbers illustrate the problem the A380 ultimately faced. The engines could be remarkably efficient for their size, but four engines still meant four sets of maintenance requirements, four nacelles, and four powerplants consuming fuel. Airbus built its last A380 in 2021, shifting the economics of the engines once again. With production over, their value increasingly depends on the aircraft that are already flying.

The end of A380 production has changed what makes a Trent 900 or GP7200 valuable. No new engine will be built for a new A380, so future revenue increasingly comes from overhauls, replacement parts, upgrades, and the engines already installed on aircraft that remain in service. The engine has effectively moved from a new-aircraft product to a long-term support business.

The size of that installed fleet still matters. According to Simple Flying's report on why Airbus offered two engines, Airbus took 251 A380 orders, 130 for GP7200-powered aircraft and 121 for the Trent 900. That left both engine families with a substantial installed base, and both manufacturers therefore have a long-running aftermarket opportunity even though the production line is closed.

Utilization will determine how long that opportunity remains attractive. The same report found Dubai hosted 29,626 A380 flights in 2026, followed by London Heathrow with 6,974 and Singapore Changi with 5,684. Concentrated flying keeps engines accumulating flight hours and reaching shop visits on a predictable schedule, exactly the type of activity that long-term service agreements such as TotalCare are designed to support. At the same time, the concentration of the remaining fleet around a relatively small number of airlines and hubs means that one operator's fleet decision can affect the economics of an entire engine family.

That makes the future value of the A380 engine more complicated than its original price tag. Airbus has not completely closed the door on the aircraft itself. As Aeroflap reported, Airbus commercial chief Christian Scherer said in July 2024 that the door was closed, "but it is not locked." Whether or not that door ever opens again, the Trent 900s and GP7200s already flying will continue generating maintenance and support revenue for as long as airlines keep their A380s in the air. The real question is no longer what one of these engines costs to buy, but how long operators will consider four of them worth paying to keep running.

We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

Can Chris Van Hollen Really Unseat Chuck Schumer?

📅 Nov 14, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The day after the Democrats notched the most impressive electoral performance by either political party in a generation, Chuck Schumer kicked off his own campaign, racing to shore up enough support to continue leading Democrats when they reclaim control of the Senate in January. His rationale was simple: He should be made majority leader because his efforts — recruiting and raising funds — created the new majority.

In podcast and television appearances, as well as an Atlantic story declaring him the “architect of a historic landslide,” Schumer made a point to emphasize his role in flipping several hard-fought seats, including in Mississippi, a state he boasted had long been his “white whale.” Schumer told told The Daily he scouted Scott Colom personally: “I went to him and said, ‘You’ve got to run.’’

But Schumer’s argument is undermined by the fact that many of the incoming senators disagree with the characterization that he contributed much to their victories at all.

Speaking to The Washington Post last week before his race was called, Colom ran through a long list of Democrats who helped him become the first Black senator to represent Mississippi since Reconstruction. Sens. Sheldon Whitehouse and Raphael Warnock, former Vice President Kamala Harris, and former President Bill Clinton were on that list; Schumer, the Post wrote, was “notably absent.” Colom was even more explicit on Friday, telling CBS it was “not accurate” to say that Schumer recruited him. “The people of Mississippi recruited me… You think I was listening to someone that lives in Brooklyn?” He declined to say whether he would back Schumer for majority leader.

Colom is not alone. Of 13 incoming Democrats, only Sherrod Brown, the once and future senator of Ohio, has publicly pledged to support the Senate’s current Democratic leader.

Abdul El-Sayed, the newly elected senator from Michigan, threw his support behind Schumer’s rival Sen. Chris Van Hollen of Maryland shortly after Van Hollen publicly announced his bid to challenge the senator from New York on Thursday. Van Hollen, who represented the D.C. suburbs in the U.S. House for a decade and half before joining the Senate in 2017, won praise in 2025 for traveling to El Salvador and demanding to meet with a constituent, Kilmar Abrego Garcia, who was erroneously sent by the Trump administration to the sprawling CECOT prison complex. Editor’s picks The 250 Greatest Albums of the 21st Century So Far The 100 Best TV Episodes of All Time The 500 Greatest Albums of All Time 100 Best Movies of the 21st Century

“I called on Sen Chris Van Hollen to lead our caucus as a candidate back in April because he leads with courage and experience. We need both right now,” El-Sayed wrote on X shortly after.

El-Sayed defeated Michigan Rep. Haley Stevens, Schumer’s preferred candidate, in the primary, before Stevens made the most dramatic heel-turn of the 2026 cycle and endorsed Republican Mike Rogers days before the election. (Schumer the move a “despicable betrayal.”)

El-Sayed is not the only new senator Schumer bet against. He backed centrist Rep. Angie Craig over Lt. Gov. Peggy Flanagan in Minnesota’s Democratic primary. Flanagan, who will take over the seat vacated by Sen. Tina Smith, has also announced her support for Van Hollen.

“Minnesotans are demanding change. Democrats need to deliver. That starts with new voices in our leadership,” Flanagan said Friday on X. “I believe Chris Van Hollen is the leader and fighter we need to bring about real change, so no American is left behind.”

Four other freshman Democrats — Sens. Troy Jackson of Maine, Juliana Stratton of Illinois, Adam Hamilton of Kansas, and Annie Andrews of South Carolina — have said explicitly they will not support Schumer, while six others have declined to say whether they would.  Related Content Kanye West Settles Lawsuit Over Sample Blocked by Descendant of Holocaust Survivors David Walliams: British Police Open Investigation Into Sexual Misconduct Allegations Trump’s Cabinet Is in Disarray Over Nvidia’s Honeypot Disaster Russell Brand: Six More Women Come Forward With Sexual-Misconduct Allegations

It is not a coincidence that Schumer’s most vocal opposition comes from recently elected senators, Ezra Levin, executive director of the progressive group Indivisible, tells Rolling Stone. “Those senators who just came through an election had to spend a lot of time with the public, and they understand where the public is [on Schumer],” Levin says.

“I don’t think anybody would deny that the people of this country want change,” adds Maurice Mitchell, national director of the Working Families Party. “People want government to do more, not less. People want immediate relief from the economic crisis that they’re in. They want accountability, and they also want new leadership. We’re listening to our folks, and we are embracing that change, and we are supporting Chris Van Hollen and his bid to be the next majority leader.”

Mitchell points to the blowout victories by newly elected Reps. Brad Lander of New York and Randy Villegas of California — primaries they won against centrist candidates closer to the Schumer mold — as evidence of the bold vision voters are looking for from Democrats. “They both ran on Medicare for All, they both ran against the war, they both were very clear about the need for us to change our relationship with the state of Israel,” Mitchell says. “Chris Van Hollen is aligned on all of those things.”

Sens. Jeff Merkley (D-Ore.) and Bernie Sanders (I-Vt.) have also thrown their support behind Van Hollen, as has Our Revolution, the political group that grew out of Sanders’ 2016 presidential run, and the Democratic Socialists of America, which kicked off a “Say No to Schumer” campaign this week.

The political advocacy group MoveOn has taken a stand against Schumer too, citing figures that indicate 82 percent of its membership wants to see him replaced. “Our members are tired of politicians content with incrementalism,” MoveOn’s Executive Director Joel Payne said in a statement. (A poll by Indivisible had worse results: just 10 percent of respondents want Schumer to remain in the leadership.)

Schumer has been regularly pilloried throughout the second Trump era for failing to do more to thwart the president’s scorched Earth agenda, provoking particular ire when he joined Republicans to pass a funding bill in March 2025. Indivisible first called on the Democratic leader to step down from his position after that vote.

“The argument, then as now, was not about ideology, it wasn’t about personalities, it wasn’t even about communication style,” Levin says. “It was about: Do you know what time it is? Do you know who is in the White House? Do you know what this regime intends to do? Are you willing to unify the caucus and strengthen the Democratic Party to get it done?”

Ahead of Van Hollen’s formal announcement on Thursday that he would seek the role, Schumer claimed that he already had the votes necessary to be reelected leader. Levin, for his part, is skeptical: “After Van Hollen announced, the fact that you did not see 29 senators, en masse, in a press release or at a press conference with Chuck Schumer means he doesn’t have the votes yet.”

Van Hollen has echoed some of the incoming senators in emphasizing that Democrats won big in the midterms in spite of, not because of, the party establishment. “I think if you look at this election, this was not a Washington, D.C.-orchestrated victory. This was a huge movement across the country,” he said this week. “That’s why we won in places like South Carolina. That’s why we won in Mississippi. Look, I appreciate the work of everybody at the DSCC, but the reality is that until the very last minute, the DSCC did not spend much in South Carolina or Mississippi.” Trending Stories Noel Fielding Says He Regrets 'Horrible' Jokes He Made With Russell Brand After Old Clip Resurfaces Pink Floyd’s Roger Waters Files for Divorce From Fourth Wife Kamilah Chavis After 5 Years of Marriage Journey Go Their Separate Ways With Longtime Drummer The ‘You Can See Everything’ Interview You’ve Been Waiting For

A total of 14 senators had publicly pledged to support Schumer on Friday, roughly half the number he would need to be reinstalled in his leadership position, a process that takes place via secret ballot. Ten senators, meanwhile, are on the record opposing his bid.

Van Hollen has acknowledged that he is an underdog in the leadership fight, but as last Tuesday’s results in places like Mississippi showed, that doesn’t mean it’s not a fight worth having.

Ronaldinho: Endrick would suit Milan or Roma, Ancelotti ‘the best coach in the world’

📅 Nov 14, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Ronaldinho feels his compatriot Endrick would be a good fit for both Milan and Roma and is confident Carlo Ancelotti will achieve ‘great things’ with Brazil: ‘He’s the best coach in the world.’

In his latest interview with Gazzetta dello Sport, Ronaldinho talked not only about his imminent return to the pitch, but also about one of his compatriots, Endrick, who has been linked with both Milan and Roma for the January window.

Endrick, 20, has made only seven appearances across all competitions for Real Madrid this season, scoring one goal against Roma.

Ronaldinho certainly believes his compatriot can leave his mark with either the Rossoneri or the Giallorossi, if he moves to Serie A in January.

“Certainly. He can play anywhere in the world,” Ronaldinho said.

“He’s played in many leagues and has done well in France, Brazil, and Spain. He’s very young, and he can do well.”

Ronaldinho is preparing to play his first competitive match in more than ten years as he’ll start for Italian Serie C side Ravenna in a derby against Forlì. The Brazilian landed in Italy on Friday and trained with his teammates for the first time today.

His return to action is part of a wider business partnership with Ravenna President Ignazio Cipriani, but Ronaldinho, who turned 46 this past March, is expected to play at least 30 minutes in an actual league game between two local rivals. His last competitive match dates back to 2015.

Another connection between Italy and Brazil is surely represented by the Seleçao coach Carlo Ancelotti: “He’s the best coach in the world; I’m sure he’ll do great things,” Ronaldinho concluded.

Air Rifles Are Still Underrated for Big Game Hunting. Here’s Why They Shouldn’t Be Overlooked

📅 Nov 14, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Published Nov 14, 2026 10:00 AM EST

We may earn revenue from the products available on this page and participate in affiliate programs. Learn More ›

The latest for gun hunters and competitive shooters.

I lived in Europe for several years and was introduced to quality airguns for competitive shooting and hunting while there. In the late 90’s I obtained my first PCP rifles, a .177 Daystate Huntsman followed by a .22 Webley Raider. It did not take long to appreciate the advantages these guns offered for small game hunting. When I returned home I found there was a small but enthusiastic community of airgun hunters testing the limits of the existing technology.

Eventually, a growing number of airgun manufacturers released larger caliber and more powerful rifles to market. It was only a matter of time before we started to think about using air rifles for larger game like deer and hogs. It was about this time that I first heard of Dennis Quackenbush and the big bore airguns he was producing, initially in .308 and .50 calibers, but eventually the .457 became his flagship model. I purchased the .308 in early 2002 for predator hunting, using it to bag fox and coyote. About the same time the idea of using these rifles for larger game really took hold with me.  But why would anyone want to use an airgun, rather than a conventional firearm?

Quite simply, because it’s fun, challenging, and effective. In the following overview, I’ll give a brief history of the big bore airgun, the motivations for hunting with them, the power they are capable of generating, hunting regulations, ammunition used for hunting, and how to keep the rifle filled with air and ready to hunt.

There are several advantages to airguns in general: they are safer to use in more developed and densely populated areas, they are far quieter than even a rimfire, and ammunition is less expensive. But in my opinion these advantages are less significant when discussing large caliber rifles. Why use them then? For many it comes down to the challenge of the hunt, the requirement for the hunter to exercise the discipline to get closer, be more selective in choosing the shot, and be more precise in shot placement. At least for me, hunting is more about how you do it, than just the outcome. Many hunters find this challenge through hunting with handguns, archery, or muzzleloaders.

In this respect hunting with a big bore airgun is closer to the ethos and field craft of bowhunting but based on the core skill set of the rifleman. And this is what many enjoy most about hunting big game with an airgun, it forces a hunter to close the range and be very precise in shot selection, with the discipline to work for the optimal shot and hold back until that is achieved.

The first commercially available rifles that were applicable for larger quarry, were the Quackenbush big bores. With the advent of this new generation of big bores, the power outputs that could be achieved with an airgun jumped from the 30-40 foot-pound of energy (fpe) range obtained with a standard .22 to over 400 fpe. And just like that, the potential for using them on deer or other large game became a reality.

If like me, you grew up hearing that a deer rifle needs to generate over 1,000 fpe to be effective, this 400 number seems very low. Well, It turns out the 1,000 fpe threshold is simply wrong. I have killed many hogs with airguns generating 100 fpe, and hogs are harder to kill than deer, in my opinion. And many times, when hunting alongside others using centerfires, my kills have been cleaner and more decisive. This is because I get in closer and place the shot more precisely. The best air rifles produce a wound similar to an arrow in that there is no hydrostatic shock generated, but rather direct injury to the vital organs. The simple fact is that 300 fpe is plenty of power to kill deer-sized game but you have to be selective with your shooting and be capable of hitting your target. You cannot be a sloppy or lazy in airgun hunting.

Today’s big bore airguns can be very powerful, and there is a wide selection of calibers and power outputs to choose from. An important factor to understand, is that the caliber is only one element contributing to the power an air rifle can generate. A .50 caliber slug in one rifle design may generate 250 fpe, but that same slug in another design can produce more than 800 fpe. The power is defined by the gun, the pressure it charges to, the pressure and volume of air it releases, and the efficiency of air flow and the guns valving system.

When talking about the more powerful rifles such as the Hatsan Piledriver, Umarex Hammer, Air Force Texan, and Western Bushbuck, you should expect to get over 600 fpe in a .457 and over 700 in the .50 caliber versions. One of the newer players in the big bore space is the AEA Zeus, which is a .72 caliber that comes in various barrel lengths and can generate between 1,000 fpe to 1,500 fpe.

Getting the rifle optimized for a hunting application is a balancing act between power, accuracy, and shot count. Some big bore airguns are regulated, for instance the Umarex Hammer has a factory set regulator adjusted to operate at 3,000 psi, and since every shot uses the same air pressure and volume it is very consistent. Some rifles, such as the AirForce Texan allow the user to adjust the power, which is usually achieved by increasing or decreasing the tension on the hammer spring. And others, such as the Western Bushbuck, have a two-step cocking position that allows either a full power or reduced power shot to be achieved. In many ways, a power adjustment on an airgun is an analog to hand loading for a powder burner.

Initially, the problem big bore airgunners faced was finding game that could be legally taken with their guns. Regulations were the barrier, and in the early days there were very few places that allowed airguns to be used for anything other than small game and pest control. Most jurisdictions have separate deer seasons for firearms, muzzleloaders, and archery that are very specific in defining the criteria for each method. And up until a few years ago, none of them specifically allowed airguns for big game. But there were a few workarounds, Texas for instance did not allow any (big or small) game animal to be taken with an airgun. However non-game species such as feral hogs, predators, and exotic species, could be taken in any number, at any time, and by any means.

Texas became ground zero for early big game airgun hunting in the U.S. The state has large populations of feral hogs, and there are also populations of feral Spanish goats, axis deer, and aoudad that could be hunted. I started flying down to the Texas to meet up with my buddy Eric Henderson, and we would roam the state hunting hogs and exotics during the day, then go after predators at night, with our Quackenbush air rifles.

We experimented with our guns, trying different fill pressures and hammer springs, to optimize power, accuracy, consistency, and the highest shot count. Somewhere along the way, big bore airguns gained visibility and more people started shooting them. This led to grassroot movements lobbying state wildlife management authorities to introduce provisions for airgun hunting, and new regulations came on the books.

At the time of this writing, several states including Michigan, Indiana, Alabama, Missouri, Georgia, Virginia, Tennessee, Kentucky, Idaho, North Dakota, and a growing number of others allow deer hunting with big bore airguns (airgun hunting law resource). Texas and Arizona not only allow deer to be taken with airguns, but most other species of big game can be taken with air powered PCP rifles as well. Some states place a caliber and/or power restriction on the guns that are permitted, so you need to check local regulations. Under these new regulations, airguns have taken whitetail and mule deer, elk, bison, bear, mountain lion, javalina, and pronghorn.

Along with the North American hunts, I’ve been traveling to South Africa for almost two decades to hunt plains game with air rifles, after being granted permission to do the first sanctioned airgun hunts there in 2003. Many of my big bores have been used to take kudu, wildebeest, blesbok, impala, springbok, warthog, bushbuck, and several other species over the years.

Many of the manufacturers producing big bore airguns offer ammunition as well. These may be produced inhouse or on an OEM basis, but in general there is a wide selection of ammo available in all big bore airgun calibers (.308-.72). Hatsan offers their own line of big bore “pellets” in .457 and .50 caliber in a variety of weights. Not surprisingly, they work very well in the Hatsan guns. Established muzzleloader companies such as Hunters Supply have an extensive line of big bore airgun slugs and pellets as well, and even the heavy hitters like Nosler have stepped in with a line of airgun ammo.

One of the companies whose product I use a lot is Mr. Hollowpoint, which is a small operation run by Robert Vogel. He was one of the early big bore airgunners and knows airguns inside and out. The slugs he produces are mostly (but not all) hollow points that are specifically designed around the individual guns.

Airguns can be notoriously finicky about the ammo they prefer, and even guns out of the same manufacturing run might have different preferences. When I get a new rifle, I will try a few slug configurations and weights until I find the one that’s most accurate—then I stick with it. Of course, if you want to use a flatter shooting 86-grain .308 slug for coyote and switch to a heavier 126-grain for better penetration on feral hogs, that’s fine. You just need to find pellets in both weights that work for your gun and commit to those.

Here’s another important consideration for all air rifle hunters: How will you keep the rifle filled? There are a couple options, but in my experience a handpump is not a viable one. The volume of air a big bore requires, the high pressures they usually fill to, and the high-volume air usage all make a handpump ineffective. This leaves you with either getting an air tank that you have filled at a paintball or dive shop, or getting your own compressor.

Tanks are a good approach; you will want to opt for a large-volume carbon fiber tank that can be filled to 4,500 psi. These tanks are connected to the rifles on board storage using a high-pressure hose with either a female quick release connector or a proprietary fill probe. The air flow circuit is opened so that the air flows from the high-pressure tank to the low-pressure onboard storage, and when the gun reaches the required pressure, the tank’s valve is closed, the bleed valve opened, the gun disconnected, and you are ready to go.

Tanks are moderately priced, fast and easy to operate, and are probably the most convenient means of filling a rifle. There is a downside though, first you need to find a place with a compressor that will fill your tanks (look for paintball shops or dive shops, which will usually do this for a fee). A bigger problem is that these shops are often unable or unwilling to charge the tank up to 4,500 psi.

Another option, especially for a high-pressure high-volume rifle is to get a portable compressor. Up until a few years ago this was cost prohibitive, unless you shot a lot or had a group of shooters going in together, as a compressor could easily cost more than $3,500. But there are several compressors (both AC and DC models) on the market today that can fill an individual gun to a 4500 psi, and they can cost less than a carbon fiber tank. The downside is they take longer to charge the gun than a tank does, they are loud, and they require some upkeep. Personally, my preference is to have both a CF tank and a portable compressor to use in combination, using the tank to charge as high as its residual pressure will allow, then topping off with the compressor.

Each of the topics we’ve covered could fill multiple chapters on their own, but now you have an honest overview big game hunting with an air rifle.

I quit hunting with anything but air-powered guns almost 15 years ago, but before that I had moved to handgun hunting and bowhunting to increase the challenge. The airgun is an effective, efficient, and ethical method of take for deer and other big game, but you need to use the gun appropriately. If you just want to go out and shoot a deer at long range and call it a season, a big bore airgun is probably not a good fit for you. However, if you want the challenge of closing the range and dropping the slug precisely on target, you need to give hunting with an air rifle a try. Now more than ever, big bore airguns are readily available. There’s a broad selection of models with a range of features and price points, and there are more places to hunt with them than ever before.

The OL editors pick the best deer hunting rifles on the market.

Jim Chapman is Outdoor Life’s top airgun contributor. He writes air gun reviews on the best hunting air rifles and shares his years of experience in airgunning with Outdoor Life readers. He lives in Minnesota with his family.
