Flight disruptions continue after FAA lifts ground stop, some college football games delayed

📅 2026-11-14  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Flight disruptions continued on Saturday and could extend through the weekend as airlines raced to recover from the Federal Aviation Administration's longest nationwide groundstop since 9/11, a measure the Trump administration blamed on a communications equipment failure in air traffic control.

More than 3,700 U.S. flights were canceled Saturday and close to 2,000 were delayed as of 9:40 a.m. ET, according to flight-tracking site FlightAware. That's on top of about 9,000 U.S. flight cancellations and more than 7,000 delays on Friday, though some disruptions were due to bad weather.

Part of the job of untangling from the ground stop includes getting aircraft where they need to be. The ground stop forced many planes to divert to other airports.

Airlines run one of the lightest schedules on Saturday and are deploying as many spare planes and crews as possible to get travelers and employees where they need to be as they're eager to start fresh for the upcoming Thanksgiving holiday period.

American Airlines, for example, is offering flight attendants extra pay to pick up trips. American and other airlines said they would change customers tickets without fee or fare differences if their plans were affected by the outage.

Around 3 million people would typically be scheduled to fly on Saturday, according to aviation data firm Cirium.

The FAA had halted departing U.S. flights around 4 p.m. on Friday, a ground stop that lasted more than four hours. Routers were overloaded with data and started giving air traffic control centers inconsistent information, so the U.S. issued the ground stop, Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy said at a news conference late Friday.

He blamed outdated U.S. infrastructure for the problem and said Congress needs to approve more money to overhaul the country's air traffic control. Aging equipment and a shortage of air traffic controllers have angered airline executives for years, who have complained about the cost of resulting disruptions.

It was the second time the FAA has issued a nationwide ground stop since the Sept. 11, 2001 terror attacks. Trump administration officials said it did not appear to be a cyberattack.

The U.S. has the world's busiest airspace.

"Before clearing flights to resume, we thoroughly tested our systems to confirm they were stable and that operations remain reliable," the FAA said late Friday.

The disruption also upended travel for NBA, NHL and college teams, which struggled to reach their destinations in time for games this weekend.

Duffy said college football teams traveling on charter flights could receive priority.

"I would encourage those football teams to reach out to the FAA to schedule their flight times and make sure they have games going off tomorrow," he said during a Friday press briefing.

The Southeastern Conference announced on X that some games had been moved to Sunday due to the flight disruption.

"We are in close communication with our member institutions to assess potential disruptions and coordinate appropriate responses," the SEC said in a statement shared on X.

The National Football league said that it is putting in three backup referres for three games on Sunday because of the ground stop, "reassigning officials to games within driving distance of their hometowns."

--CNBC's Jessica Golden contributed to this article.

The FAA‘s issuance of a nationwide ground stoppage has come on the eve of one of the entertainment industry’s busiest weekends, as organizers scramble to determine if attendees will be able to make it to the events.

One of the most high-profile events, the Academy of Motion Picture Arts & Sciences’ 17th Governors Awards, a must-attend for every A-lister in the industry with more than 225 invited guests for this year’s show, is scheduled for Sunday at the Ray Dolby Ballroom at Ovation in Hollywood. 6 best and worst fantasy football matchups for Week 10

📅 Nov 14, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The fantasy football playoffs start in just over a month, making each remaining week critical for success.

This week, we once again have four bye weeks to worry about (Bears, Broncos, Buccaneers, Eagles), as well.

Every week, I'll continue to identify and write up players that are not obviously starts or sits. Regardless of matchup, you're starting someone like Christian McCaffrey every week, so I try to go a few layers deeper than your early-round fantasy studs.

Note: Projections refer to player props in the betting and prediction markets, which are a valuable reference tool for anyone playing fantasy football.

Through three games, Shough was the QB3 overall with 24 fantasy points per game. Since, Shough has given you just 16 fantasy points per game, good for QB17 during that stretch.

The past three games were particularly tough for Shough managers, as he faced the Steelers and Browns, with a bye week jammed between.

The good news? It's time to insert Shough back into your fantasy lineup with confidence.

Week 3: Denver Broncos cheerleaders perform during the "Sunday Night Football" game against the Los Angeles Rams at Empower Field at Mile High.

Week 3: The Maracanã Stadium gets set to host the International Series game between the Baltimore Ravens and Dallas Cowboys in Rio de Janeiro, Brazil.

Week 1: Kim Thayil of Soundgarden and Mike McCready of Pearl Jam perform during halftime of the 2026 NFL Kickoff Game between the Seattle Seahawks and New England Patriots at Lumen Field.

Week 3: Denver Broncos cheerleaders perform during the "Sunday Night Football" game against the Los Angeles Rams at Empower Field at Mile High.

This matchup with Carolina has a hefty 48.5-point game total, and the Saints are slight 2.5-point favorites. That suggests a tight game with shootout potential.

Shough's pass yards projection here is 251.5 yards, while multiple passing touchdowns is currently juiced in betting markets, signaling that Vegas expects a strong passing performance overall. For context, Jordan Love's pass yards/pass touchdowns combination is worse than Shough's this week.

Each week, I stress that we should opponent-adjust when doing matchups analysis, and the Panthers are the perfect example of this. Your fantasy app likely shows Shough as having a below average matchup this week, though Carolina is actually one of the more favorable matchups once we opponent-adjust.

Four touchdowns to Caleb Williams in Week 1

20 fantasy points to Deshaun Watson in Week 3

More than 400 passing yards to Jared Goff in Week 4

288 passing yards and three touchdowns to Jordan Love in Week 8

237 yards and two touchdowns to the struggling Bo Nix in Week 9

There's really just three outlier games that make Carolina's total fantasy points allowed to quarterbacks a respectable number:

The Falcons duo of Cooper Rush and Jack Strand combined for negative fantasy points in Week 2

Tanner McKee struggled against Carolina in his first game of 2026

The Buccaneers ran for over 250 yards in Week 7, so Baker Mayfield only threw for 64 yards

In short, fire up Shough as a top-eight fantasy option against the Panthers this week.

Seattle draws the second-best rushing matchup of Week 10 against Las Vegas, a defense allowing a tasty 5.0 yards per carry to opposing backs. It's also the first start of Fernando Mendoza's career, so there's an elevated chance of a couple turnovers that result in great field position for the Seahawks.

The Seahawks are touchdown favorites, so game script should be favorable for the Seahawks running back duo. Looking at Charbonnet and Jadarian Price, the veteran has quickly taken over this backfield.

In Week 9, Charbonnet carried 16 times for 77 yards, while the rookie Price had just 24 yards on nine carries. While Price did have a slick four catches for 54 receiving yards in that game, Charbonnet actually ran almost twice as many routes, so we should expect bigger receiving games from the veteran moving forward.

Turning to the betting markets, Charbonnet is nearly 50-50 to score in this contest, and his total yards prop is quite high at 76.5 yards.

Consider Charbonnet a top-15 fantasy back in Week 10, as he should flirt with 20 touches against one of the more beatable run defenses in Las Vegas.

The second-year receiver draws the best outside matchup of Week 10 against the Saints.

Cornerbacks Kool-Aid McKinstry and Martin Emerson have been getting cooked all season, and they most recently gave up over 200 yards on the outside to a considerably worse Browns passing attack.

The Saints pass rush has not gotten to the quarterback this year, as they are bottom-10 in pressure rate. Bryce Young, when given time, has been one of the NFL's best passers in 2026. When pressured, he turns into one of the worst.

McMillan already has three games this year with at least 10 targets, and he has a very strong chance to make that four games after this week. He's a fringe top-12 fantasy option at wide receiver.

You already know to start tight ends against the Lions every week, right? Good. I don't want to detail that matchup every article.

Anyway, Pitts has the third-best matchup this week against a Kansas City defense that has struggled to contain passes over the middle of the field. Pitts isn't always used like a classic tight end, which is why it's worth mentioning that the Chiefs have been vulnerable to slot receivers this season.

The game total here is reasonably high at 46.5 points, and the Falcons are only three-point underdogs. This could be a sneaky shootout game environment.

While it's been back-to-back quiet games for Pitts, the athletic tight end has still earned at least five targets in each of the past five games.

Think of Pitts as a back-end fantasy TE1 this week with serious upside given the matchup.

Pollard has been on a tear, with at least 135 total yards and multiple touchdowns in Weeks 7 and 8, prior to the team's Week 9 bye. Unfortunately, Pollard should remain parked on your fantasy bench in Week 10 against Jacksonville.

The Jaguars were already the best run defense in the NFL, and then they added DT Jordan Davis from the Eagles. The 6-foot-6, 336-pounder is one of the NFL's best run stoppers, and it's borderline unfair to pair with him Travon Walker, Josh Hines-Allen, and Arik Armstead.

The Titans are more than 10-point underdogs, and while the Titans are well-rested after their bye, the Jaguars are coming off a long break thanks to a cake walk on Thursday night against Joe Fagnano's Ravens.

I'd rather start the aforementioned Charbonnet or Woody Marks against a surprisingly beatable Browns run defense. Consensus rankings generally have Pollard higher than those two, and I'm here to tell you the public is wrong this week.

Jacoby Brissett is now on the Ravens and Gardner Minshew is undergoing season-ending elbow surgery. Round three rookie Carson Beck is suddenly thrust into NFL action against a tough Rams pass defense. And, star receiver Michael Wilson is also now out for the season due to a neck fracture.

As a result, the whole Cardinals offense should suffer, and their Vegas team total of 15 points is historically low. Allgeier's touchdown odds of 33% are hilariously low for a starting back projected for around 20 touches, and his total yards prop is just 56.5 yards.

For context, Steelers backup Rico Dowdle is projected for 49.5 total yards and a 36% chance of a touchdown. And I think we'd all agree that Dowdle is off the fantasy radar in Week 10.

Last week was the first time the Rams had a healthy Aaron Donald and Myles Garrett. The Commanders backs mustered just 44 yards on 15 carries, and overall Jayden Daniels and crew managed just 13 points.

Allgeier should be inefficient on his touches and probably won't score. He is outside the top-24 fantasy backs in Week 10 as a result.

Rinnai’s new heat pump water heaters get smarter – and easier to install

📅 Nov 14, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Rinnai has launched a new lineup of electric heat pump water heaters that adds wifi controls and more installation options to an appliance that can cut the energy needed for hot showers.

The REHPW series comes in 50-, 65-, and 80-gallon sizes. It builds on Rinnai’s existing REHP lineup, with changes designed to make the units easier to install, monitor, and maintain.

Heat pump water heaters pull heat from the surrounding air and transfer it into the tank. Moving that heat requires less electricity than producing it with conventional electric resistance elements. There’s no fuel combustion at the appliance, although emissions from its electricity supply depend on the grid.

Rinnai lists a Uniform Energy Factor, or UEF, of 3.75 for the 50-gallon model and 4.0 for the two larger versions. Higher numbers mean greater efficiency under standardized testing; actual bills depend on electricity rates, hot water use, and installation conditions.

The lineup delivers up to 91 gallons of hot water in the first hour, depending on tank size. That rating includes both stored hot water and what the unit can heat during that hour – useful context when several people take consecutive showers.

There are five operating modes, 4.5 kW backup heating elements, and a variable-speed fan designed to keep noise down. The heat pump can operate with ambient air as low as 37°F.

Installation is where some of the more practical upgrades show up.

The new Rinnai heat pump water heater units have water connections on both the top and side, giving installers more options for connecting existing plumbing. Both the air intake and exhaust are on top, allowing zero side clearance where Rinnai’s installation requirements permit.

An optional ducting kit adds flexibility for tighter spaces. Carrying handles help with moving the tank into place, and the air filter slides out horizontally from the side for cleaning.

Rinnai has also added a third tank temperature sensor to the 50- and 80-gallon models to help manage hot water delivery.

Wifi adds remote monitoring and control. Rinnai’s updated Central app supports the REHPW series, including scheduling and tools to use time-of-use electricity rates to manage energy use.

The units also carry CTA-2045 AC Level 2 certification for demand response. That gives compatible utility programs a way to communicate with the water heater and adjust its electricity use. Rinnai says the certification can help qualify homeowners for local and state utility demand-response rebates; availability depends on the program.

The series is Energy Star certified, meets NEEA Tier 4 requirements, and complies with California’s Title 24 requirements, including JA13, according to Rinnai. Warranty coverage includes 10 years for the tank and other parts, plus one year for labor.

Rinnai directs buyers to an installer for pricing, so the upfront cost – and how much a local rebate might offset it – will require a quote. However, the REHP series price on Amazon hovers around the $2,000 mark.

Painter Sascha Braunig Pushes Through the Void

📅 Nov 14, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Sascha Braunig’s paintings have a tendency to produce visceral reactions: a pit in the stomach, an eyebrow raise, a need to move closer. This is not unintended.

“I want the viewer to have a bodily experience,” Braunig says.

The paintings are bright, smooth, candy-colored—lighthearted contrasts to the psychologically intense shapes: luminously rendered heads and torsos often bound by wiry tentacles. At times the figures recede into their matching optic backgrounds, as if hiding in plain sight.

Braunig, who is 43 and splits her time between New York and Maine, has been painting with this funky verve for close to two decades. Not long after she got her MFA from Yale’s painting program in 2008, she was having solo exhibitions of her small paintings of heads at the now-defunct Foxy Production in Manhattan. Inclusions at the New Museum Triennial and MoMA PS1 soon followed. (Attention from critics came quickly as well; one called her an “inspired reanimator of Surrealism,” another a “phenom.”)

Braunig is excellent at capturing the inherent tension of living in a body—especially one that is subjected to societal expectations of femininity. She’s playing with the ways women have been contorted and cinched and squeezed for time immemorial, asked to fit into archetypes that seem at turns glamorous or sinister. Often they are both.

“Our aesthetic, our personhood is formed in relation to these cultural forces and imagery,” she says. “You can’t separate yourself out from what you’ve seen throughout your whole life.”

These themes of gender performance and corporeal control permeate “Wrist Painter,” Braunig’s most extensive exhibition to date, which opens November 13 at the Portland Art Museum. The show brings together some 45 works, including paintings, sculptures, works on paper, and sketchbooks. It will be her first solo institutional show in Maine. (A special occasion, as almost all of the pieces in the show were made a block and a half away, in her studio at the Portland arts nonprofit SPACE, over the last 15 years.)

“Sascha really stands out here in this arts ecosystem. She’s doing something really singular,” says Sayantan Mukhopadhyay, a curator at PMA who organized the show. When people think of Maine painters, they usually think of landscapes. But Braunig leans toward the cerebral, with a bit more bite. “The paradoxes are what really excite me about the work,” Mukhopadhyay says.

And the dichotomies are many. “Sascha has a way of presenting an abstraction that can read both soft and strong, feminine and violent,” says Olivia Smith, whose New York City gallery, Magenta Plains, now represents Braunig. “I think that’s what I’ve always loved: that she offers a way for the viewer to re-associate, which ends up telling them more about themselves than about Sascha.”

One of the earliest artworks in “Wrist Painter” is Sequins, from 2010. It’s from Braunig’s head series, which she began in grad school. Her process starts with a drawing, then she brings her sketch to life by making an embellished three-dimensional model. Then she’ll light the model and paint what she observes. She applies her lustrous oil paints with such precision that these strange bodily shapes start to look “real.”

She continued this system as she moved on to her bodies series a few years later, using clay resin, wire, and paper to make her maquettes. This haptic process is why works like Warm Leatherette, a 2015 painting at PMA that looks like a snatched Michelin Man encased in chartreuse padding, seem so tactile. (She has often described her choice of colors as “seasonal” or “dietary”—she finds a palette that works for one show and then cycles to a new one for the next.)

Her maquette process also lends a certain theatricality. She is literally setting up a scene, as if the entire idea of femininity were on a velvet-curtained stage, the whole specter aglow under klieg lights. What is gender if not a performance?

Braunig, who grew up in British Columbia, Canada, liked drawing and painting from a young age. Her father (a dentist) and mother (a nurse) enjoyed creative pursuits outside of work, and the house was full of art books. She loved watching Pee-wee’s Playhouse, which she credits as being just as formative, even more so, than going to museums.

Her parents encouraged her to attend an intensive summer art program at Skidmore College in upstate New York when she was 16. It was there that she first learned about Cooper Union, the New York City school where artists Lee Krasner and Wangechi Mutu got their start. Braunig decided it was the place for her.

New York City was the perfect place for the budding artist. “I was totally in love. It maybe ruined me forever,” she jokes now. She went to Yale right after Cooper, and then she moved to Maine in 2010, in her mid-20s. “I was really fortunate to have a New York gallery as I left the city, because I was able to have shows and have my work represented here even though I wasn’t here.”

Living somewhere far less expensive was also a game changer—she didn’t need to pile on jobs to make ends meet, and could instead focus on building her deeply personal style. “I had been exposed to so many influences coming to New York. And maybe those first five years in Maine were a time when I was able to process them alone.”

Braunig knows her art history, and she gets why people connect her style back to Surrealism. But her artistic comrades are more like French self-portraitist Claude Cahun or even Frida Kahlo, she says, “who’s really making work that is influenced by events of her own life.” Her art might feel dreamy (or perhaps like an acid trip), but “it’s not a dream space. I really am thinking about actual forces in the actual world.”

For many years Braunig used her work to illustrate broader ideas around culture and gender, but her most recent output has taken a turn toward the autobiographical. A few years ago she went through a creative block that left her stranded from her art practice, unable to make anything. As she emerged from this period last year, she started making her most personal paintings yet.

That work went into her radiant show “Cauldron” at Magenta Plains this past spring, its title a reference to the messy, transformative stew of midlife. Its anchor painting was My Death (2025), which, along with several others from the show, will travel to Portland for “Wrist Painter.”

Sascha Braunig, My Death, 2025, oil on linen over panel.

She calls My Death an “explicit self-portrait” about her own reanimation—an emergence through the void of creative block. It’s juicy and haunting and beautiful. She employed those tentacles again, but this time they are like a life force attached to a sleeping Braunig. A mysterious figure made from strips of vermilion orange pulls on them like marionette strings, bringing the artist back to life.

“I’ve come to understand that painting is a survival practice,” she says. “That can be a framing practice where you’re using it to talk about ideas in the world, or it can be a practice where you’re directly processing your own experiences. Maybe those two things aren’t that different.”

For the PMA show, Braunig has made one brand-new work, titled Torsion. At 78 inches tall by 60 inches wide, it is the largest painting of her career. She previously used the title for a museum show in Norway, but realized she’d never given it to an individual painting. The term, in physics, refers to the twisting of an object around an axis: a towel wrung out to dry.

This new Torsion is a painting of a fleshy pink figure with a tiny, twisted torso standing on tiptoes within a gray box. The figure’s muscular back is to the viewer, its knees fanning out in a plié. Dramatic tentacles in orange and yellow trace its exaggerated outline.

The figure’s head is cut off by the box’s ceiling, and its arms stop around the elbows, where they encounter the side walls. Maybe the figure’s been blocked, held back by the confines of this shadowy encasement. Or maybe, somehow, it has broken free.

“Sascha Braunig: Wrist Painter” is on view at the Portland Art Museum in Maine from November 13, 2026, through March 14, 2027.

HBO’s 5-Part Crime Drama Has One of the Best Seasons in TV History

📅 Nov 14, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The Wire’s fourth season provided a far deeper look at Baltimore’s problems

The Wire’s fourth season provided a far deeper look at Baltimore’s problems

At the turn of the 21st century, HBO was the place for prestige crime drama. The launch of The Sopranos in 1999 paved the way for the 2002 premiere of The Wire, which focused on drug dealers in Baltimore and the police officers trying to disrupt their operations. The series is still one of the best shows of all time, and provided breakout roles for huge stars like Idris Elba and Michael B. Jordan. But while it was compelling from the beginning, The Wire truly achieved perfection in season 4 when the scope expanded beyond the drug trade to examine the cycle of institutional failure.

Season 1 of The Wire was tightly focused on the drug empire of Avon Barksdale (Wood Harris) and his business-savvy second-in-command Stringer Bell (Elba). Showrunner David Simon quickly established a brilliant mix of humor and pathos. The show could find comedy in something as simple as cops struggling to get new furniture into their office, and then gut audiences with the death of a relatable drug dealer. Season 2 shifted the focus to the city’s port system, a jarring change as many characters faded into the background to make room for the tragic tale of the Sobotka family. Season 3 was the most tightly linked to the show’s first season, largely completing the story of the Barksdale gang.

That made season 4 perfect for a soft reboot, a shift that was defined by star Dominic West all but disappearing after his relentless detective, Jimmy McNulty, was demoted for pissing off his bosses too many times. The detectives he previously worked with in the major crimes unit continue their work, investigating a vicious new gang that’s filling the void Barksdale left behind. Lester Freamon (Clarke Peters) effectively steps into the McNulty role of pursuing the truth, even when his supervisors would love for him to be a little less dogged, bringing a new methodical nature to the investigations instead of McNulty’s brash confidence. But while the cops vs. criminals beats are familiar, they become just one part of a far more ambitious season that fully capitalized on Simon’s knowledge of the city. (Simon worked as a journalist at The Baltimore Sun for 12 years before creating The Wire.)

The season’s largest secondary plot involves the students at a Baltimore middle school where former cop Roland Pryzbylewski (Jim True-Frost) begins teaching. The young actors do a phenomenal job establishing likable characters in terrible circumstances. You want to root for all of them, from the ambitious Randy Wagstaff (Maestro Harrell) to the fiercely protective Michael Lee (Tristan Wilds). By the end of season 4, the system has profoundly failed almost all of these kids, driving them to lives of addiction and violence. The Wire was always good at making most of its characters sympathetic, but seeing children trapped in such a brutal cycle really drives home the show’s themes of how crime thrives when institutions fail.

That theme also plays out in the mayoral campaign of Tommy Carcetti (Aidan Gillen). Carcetti was introduced in season 3 as an ambitious city council member, but he really shines in season 4 as he runs on reforming the Baltimore Police Department — and then quickly breaks his promises. Gillen’s oily performance as a political survivor who would doom his city to satisfy his own ambitions was what landed him the role of Littlefinger in Game of Thrones. Carcetti is even more compelling a character because he feels like a man who could have done good if he’d been willing to make sacrifices rather than feed his own ego.

The Wire season 4 is able to build so many new arcs because of the strong foundation established in the previous three seasons. The show is dense with rich characters who have grown significantly over the years even as they continue to struggle with many of the same problems. The cops and drug dealers alike feel world-weary as they deal with a more violent new generation represented by Marlo Stanfield (Jamie Hector) and his vicious enforcers Snoop (Felicia Pearson) and Chris Partlow (Gbenga Akinnagbe). Combined with the dark fate of most of the kids and Carcetti’s broken promises, the season creates the bleak feeling that everyone is trapped in a cycle of misery and decay.

Season 5 gave most of The Wire’s characters satisfying ends to their arcs and delivered a scathing critique of the downfall of American newspapers, commentary that has only become more profound 20 years later. But overall, it’s a big step down from season 4, spending too much time on a goofy fake serial killer plot and not enough on the nuanced character-driven stories that made season 4 feel so authentic. There are no bad seasons of The Wire, but season 4 perfectly achieved the show’s goals. It’s an alternately funny, tragic, and profound look at the complex ways that people are betrayed by systems that never really give them a chance to succeed.

Trump doesn’t plan to raise Nvidia, spy issue during China trip: U.S. officials

📅 2026-11-14  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

President t Donald Trump is not expected to raise allegations of a Chinese intelligence operation involving former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang during his upcoming meeting with Chinese President Xi Jinping, senior U.S. officials said Friday.

Officials also said that Elon Musk, the CEO of Tesla and SpaceX, will accompany Trump on the trip to Shenzhen, China, for the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation summit on Nov. 18 and 19.

The White House, earlier Friday, said Trump plans to have a three-way summit there with Xi and Russian President Vladimir Putin.

Jensen Huang's relationship with the suspected spy, Alice Huang, who is not related to him, has raised concerns that a Singapore-based company where she was an executive diverted restricted Nvidia chips to China.

"The situation with Nvidia should not really have any bearing on the trip," an official said on the press call, speaking on the condition that they not be identified.

"In all of our discussions and negotiations with the Chinese, we do not negotiate American export controls ... and we don't expect it to figure at all in any of our conversations with the Chinese while we're there."

Asked if Trump would raise the allegations against Alice Huang to Chinese leader Xi Jinping, the official said, "The focus of a meeting with President Xi, at least on the economic side, is going to be about implementation of the trade agreement."

"When it comes to some of these national security matters and examinations and investigations that are going on in the U.S., [that is] probably not something that we're going to read the Chinese in on at this moment," the official said.

Another official said, "I don't expect the president to raise it — the Alice Huang issue."

China has called the allegations against Alice Huang baseless.

Huang resigned this week as Nvidia's CEO after damning reports that he had an affair with Alice Huang, who was an executive of Megaspeed, a cloud-computing company that is being eyed for possible facilitation of sending billions of dollars worth of Nvidia chips in violation of U.S. restrictions on those chips.

The scandal has rocked Nvidia and raised questions about whether the Commerce Department, which is led by Howard Lutnick, has been aggressive enough at blocking advanced chips from ending up in China.

During Friday's call, a reporter asked why Musk was going on the trip with Trump.

"I don't want to speak for the president on exactly why Elon's going," an official said.

"Obviously, he has tremendous business interests in the U.S.-China relationship. He's a big part of our national security," the official said. "There are numerous reasons, given the engagement that the president is going to have, where I think Elon would be of tremendous benefit, just even as an advisor to the president."

FAA grounds departing flights nationwide, citing air traffic communication system outage

📅 2026-11-13  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The Federal Aviation Administration halted thousands of departing flights nationwide on Friday afternoon, a rare measure, blaming communications issues that affected "multiple" air traffic control centers. More than 10,000 flights were disrupted.

Homeland Security Secretary Markwayne Mullin said Friday at the White House that there was no indication that the issues were a threat to the United States.

The FAA said in post on X that there was a "temporary telecommunications outage" and that it halted flights out of an "abundance of caution." The issues were related to communications and radar problems, according to an internal message from Alaska Airlines, which was seen by CNBC.

Airlines said en route flights would continue, but carriers were preparing to cancel flights as the disruption wore on. JetBlue Airways told staff it would cancel the remainder of the day's flights, aside from European routes, as well as others scheduled for Saturday so that crews and planes are in place to resume operations, according to a memo seen by CNBC.

FAA administrator Bryan Bedford had a call with airline CEOs around 6 p.m. indicating that it was unclear when the situation would be resolved, according to people familiar with the matter.

As of 5:56 p.m. ET, some outbound international traffic was in the air, according to Flightradar24, flight-tracking site. That included a Lufthansa flight from Boston to Frankfurt, Germany, that departed at 5:21 p.m. ET and an ITA Airways flight from Boston to Rome that left at 5:24 p.m. ET, according to the site.

As of 6:50 p.m. ET, cargo and passenger flights were also departing the West Coast for Asia and Hawaii.

Flights will be allowed to depart as en route traffic facilities recover, "through coordination with the air traffic control system," the FAA said at about 6 p.m..

Airlines said flights in the air would continue.

When it issued the ground stop at about 4 p.m., the FAA said the communications issues were "impacting multiple Air Route Traffic Control Centers." These facilities guide airplanes at high altitudes along their routes.

The FAA's technicians are investigating, the agency said.

A ground stop halts flights at their departure point. Disruptions tend to snowball if ground stops drag on as flight crews reach their maximum number of duty hours for a day.

There were 11,000 flights scheduled to depart U.S. airports after 4 p.m. ET on Friday, according to flight-data firm Cirium. There were about 600 diverted flights on Friday, 104 of them sent to other airports, Cirium said.

Nationwide ground stops are extremely rare.

In January 2023, the FAA halted flights because of issues with a pilot alert system. Before that, traffic was halted nationwide after the Sept. 11, 2001, terror attacks.

Technical issues have repeatedly snarled air traffic in the U.S. in recent years.

In September, hundreds of flights into Newark, New Jersey, and Philadelphia were canceled, delayed or diverted after a Verizon fiber cable was accidentally cut. Officials said it occurred during construction in New Jersey, severing a backup feed of key data for air traffic controllers.

In 2025, air traffic controllers lost contact with aircraft heading to and from Newark Liberty International Airport, an equipment failure that led to massive flight delays and raised more concerns about aging U.S. aviation infrastructure and staffing shortages.

It wasn't immediately clear what caused Friday's problem.

Inbound international flights will be managed on a case by case basis, the FAA said.

The major U.S. airlines issued statements saying they would work with customers who were affected by the ground stop and encouraged travelers to check for updates.

— CNBC's Kevin Breuninger and Phil LeBeau contributed to this article.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/providers/ardent-health-acquiring-missouri-nonprofit-boone-health-47m" hreflang="en">Ardent Health acquiring Missouri nonprofit Boone Health for $47M</a>

📅 2026-11-13  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →
Ardent Health acquiring Missouri nonprofit Boone Health for $47M" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Ardent Health is acquiring Boone Health, a mid-sized nonprofit system based in Columbia, Missouri, for $47 million in cash, the organizations announced Thursday. It marks Ardent's first entry into the Missouri healthcare market.

The deal, expected to close in the first quarter of 2027, includes a 10-year, $200 million capital commitment on nonprofit Ardent’s part. That money will be invested in Boone’s facilities, workforce and, in the near term, an expansion of its emergency department, according to the announcement.

Ardent has agreed to maintain Boone’s essential hospital services while developing its 392-bed regional referral center, Boone Hospital Center. It also plans to maintain employment for existing Boone employees and providers, continue its charity care policy and establish a local advisory board.

“We are acquiring a strong asset in an attractive and growing midsize market with significant opportunity to drive operating and financial improvement, all while delivering high patient quality care and safety,” Ardent Health President and CEO Dave Caspers said during a Thursday afternoon investor call discussing the deal. “It also represents an effective use of capital at a compelling valuation that we expect will create long-term shareholder value.”

Alongside its main hospital, Ardent would acquire Boone’s medical group, 19 care sites including an outpatient campus and five medical office buildings, related ancillary services and real estate. The system is the second-largest provider in its region with about 30% market share and half a billion dollars of annual revenue, Caspers said, with a local population that is well insured (93% coverage) and growing well ahead of the national average (3.4% in the next five years).

“Boone expands our footprint into a new attractive market,” Caspers explained. “It diversifies our business and enhances scale. Further, adding Boone's regional system to our portfolio is consistent with our strategy of building leading healthcare platforms in growing mid-sized communities. We're highly aligned with Boone Health on culture and mission, including putting the patient at the center of everything we do, with an aim toward best-in-class clinical care, patient quality, safety and access to services.”

The executive noted to investors that Boone’s facilities are relatively modern and well maintained, allowing Ardent to direct more capital toward its growth. The company has also identified “meaningful opportunities to optimize financial performance.” By leveraging its larger scale and expertise to tackle costs (supply chain procurement, vendor consolidation) and revenue cycle processes, Ardent executives said they expect to generate about $5 million to $10 million additional EBITDA within the deal’s first year, and at least $30 million of annual synergies three years after the deal’s close.

Boone Health has been independent since 2021, following over three decades during which it operated as part of the BJC HealthCare system. In 2025, its leadership kicked off a search for potential strategic partners “that could provide additional capital and resources to support the system's continued growth. Ardent was selected after a comprehensive evaluation process led by an independent advisory firm,” according to Thursday’s announcement.

“In Ardent, we identified a partner that supports our mission, respects our culture and can accelerate our vision without compromising what makes Boone Health special,” Jerry Kennett, M.D., chair of Boone’s Board of Trustees, said in the announcement. "Following a thoughtful review, the Board is confident Ardent is the right long-term partner for Boone Health. This agreement protects what our community has built while giving Boone Health the resources it needs to grow."

Ardent Health’s current operations include 30 acute care hospitals and over 280 care sites, several of which are run as part of a joint venture structure with other health system partners. In 2025, the company increased its revenues by 6% to $6.3 billion and grew its adjusted EBITDA to $545 million.

The company recently logged a strong third quarter off the back of solid demand for emergent care and progress on its ongoing performance improvement initiative. In September, it also officially divested from a joint venture with the University of Kansas Health System, a decision that leadership said is part of an ongoing review of its portfolio to ensure long-term strategic fit among its hospitals and partnerships.

Serie A, 320 milioni da Avenue Capital ma alcuni club frenano: ecco perché l'accordo è a rischio

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Serie A, 320 milioni da Avenue Capital ma alcuni club frenano: ecco perché l'accordo è a rischio

Installa la nostra App gratuita e accedi più velocemente alle notizie

Mentre navighi nell'app, scorri il dito da sinistra a destra dello schermo per tornare alle pagine precedenti

Si complica la trattativa tra la Lega Serie A e Avenue Capital per la cessione di una quota del business dei diritti televisivi internazionali. Secondo quanto riportato da Reuters, alcuni club del massimo campionato italiano avrebbero espresso perplessità sull'operazione, rallentando il percorso verso un possibile accordo.

Il fondo statunitense, con sede a New York, ha messo sul tavolo 320 milioni di euro per acquisire circa il 10% della nuova società destinata a gestire i diritti internazionali della Serie A, comprese le attività legate alle trasmissioni televisive, alle sponsorizzazioni e al settore delle scommesse. Una proposta che attribuisce alla società una valutazione complessiva di 3,2 miliardi di euro.

Non tutti i club, però, sono convinti di concedere ad Avenue Capital l'esclusiva nelle trattative. Secondo tre fonti interpellate da Reuters, alcune società ritengono che la Lega debba mantenere la possibilità di esaminare eventuali offerte alternative mentre approfondisce la proposta del fondo statunitense.

Al momento Avenue Capital rimane l'unico soggetto attivamente impegnato nel negoziato, pur senza aver ottenuto formalmente l'esclusiva. Le fonti non hanno specificato quali siano i club contrari né il loro numero, ma le resistenze emerse sarebbero sufficienti a rallentare l'operazione.

Per arrivare all'approvazione definitiva sarà necessario ottenere il voto favorevole di almeno 14 delle 20 società di Serie A.

Non sarebbe la prima volta che il calcio italiano tenta di aprire agli investitori esterni. Nel 2021 era stata valutata la cessione del 10% del business dei diritti televisivi nazionali a un consorzio guidato da CVC, ma il progetto era naufragato per la mancanza del consenso necessario tra le società.

Anche questa volta, dunque, la posizione dei club sarà determinante. L'offerta di Avenue Capital rimane sul tavolo, ma l'intesa è tutt'altro che raggiunta.

Personal Finance (Not Investing) • Re: Testamentary Trust - Making sure money goes to kids, not a new spouse

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

I did not see poster mention it was a QTIP trust. Merely a “grantor” trust, which could be a revocable living trust.
You're right, he said "grantor trust"... but that only tells us how it was structured for income taxes while Dad was alive. What I'm wondering is, what the terms were for when his father died? Indeed, it might be a simple survivor's trust (step-mother controls everything), and if so, then his Option 2 (Dad was incompetent) is true. But I think he should check, not assume. For example, why would they be talking about annuities that "didn't get moved to the trust by accident"-- meaning, I assume, that the beneficiary designation wasn't changed from his step-mother to the trust-- if the trust is a survivor trust? The lawyer wouldn't have told you to do that, if it was all going to be controlled by his step-mother anyway?

All estates attorneys have had testators insist up and down that everybody is on the same page, they have a tight, loving family, no worries at all about that stuff... and then seen things go off like a bomb even before the funeral is over. The attorney's job is to protect their clients from things like this. And from potential creditors. And from tax law changes lowering the estate tax threshold, or if the client later moves to a state with a lower limit (... or they get a windfall, and now it's an issue). And from beneficiaries who develop drug problems, marry/procreate with dirtbags, get sued, develop dementia, etc. Nobody likes to think this is going to happen to their family, but it does happen to families, and some text in the documents up front will protect the client's interests if it does, and does no harm to anyone if it doesn't. This is why you pay a lawyer to set things up for you.

Given that the clients' desires were, his half of the estate goes to his three biological kids, her half of the estate goes to her two biological kids, I would expect the lawyer to start from an A/B trust at least, and given that CA is known as a litigious state, possibly the stronger protections from a QTIP. There would also have to be some language about successor trustees, and a natural choice would be replacing Dad with one of his biological kids, Mom with one of hers. Maybe they asked the lawyer to do something else, maybe not. So... he should take a good look at the documents, and then he'll know. (And also, BTW, make sure that the appropriate paperwork is filed with the IRS, there's a 9 month limit I believe...)

CHZ is testing the lower boundary of a long term descending channel near $0.0100-$0.01.59.

The first upside targets are $0.027 and $0.057, followed by $0.15 and $0.33.

The $0.95 level remains a distant chart target, conditional on a sustained bullish reversal.

The Chiliz price is sitting in a spot that deserves attention, but not blind optimism. On the weekly chart for Binance CHZ/USDT, the token is pressing against the lower boundary of a long running descending channel. That area has acted as a buying zone before. Whether buyers show up again is the question now.

The chart traces a broad downward channel from the 2021 peak, with price repeatedly making lower swings inside its boundaries. The latest move has brought CHZ back toward the channel floor and a marked support zone around $0.0100-$0.01059. A green band on the chart extends to roughly $0.013, highlighting the area being watched.

That’s a location, not a confirmed reversal. The setup may offer an attractive risk reward profile on paper, but the chart alone doesn’t prove that demand will hold. A sustained loss of this floor would weaken the bullish case.

$CHZ Channel Bottom Attracting Buyers 👩‍💻Chiliz is trading near the lower border of the descending channel formation on the weekly timeframe – right where bulls have historically stepped in.The technical setup is increasingly promising for buyers in this zone, and the… pic.twitter.com/LYKwyztfPu

If support holds and momentum turns, the chart marks $0.027 as the first upside target, followed by $0.057. Beyond those levels, resistance markers sit at $0.15 and $0.33, while $0.95 is the distant upper target. Those are chart projections, not guaranteed destinations. CHZ would need to reclaim each area rather than teleport there of enthusiasm.

The descending channel’s upper boundary also matters. A move above it would provide a stronger technical signal than simply bouncing from support.

For now, the Chiliz price story is a test of the floor. Buyers have a clearly defined area to defend, while traders have equally clear levels to monitor overhead. Until price confirms a bounce or breaks support, calling a bottom would be getting ahead of the evidence. The chart is interesting, confirmation is still missing.

Iraq's oil output climbed to its highest level in seven years, the IEA said

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

Gulf crude exports reached 18.4 million barrels a day in October, still 26% below pre-war levels, the IEA said

Iraq pumped 4.6 million barrels of crude a day in October, an increase of 540,000 barrels a day from the previous month, the International Energy Agency said in its monthly oil market report on Friday.

That was the highest level since September 2019, according to Bloomberg.

Iraq's output ran 170,000 barrels a day above its implied target of 4.43 million, the IEA said. The agency put the country's sustainable capacity at 4.87 million barrels a day.

Gulf producers excluding Oman exported 18.4 million barrels a day in October, which is 26% below pre-war levels, the IEA said. Those exports rose by 950,000 barrels a day from the prior month, according to Bloomberg. Producers in the region are sending some cargoes on low-profile tanker runs across the Strait of Hormuz and are shipping other crude by alternative export routes that never touch the strait.

Global oil supply rose by 2.3 million barrels a day to 105.4 million in October, the IEA said. That followed a 3 million barrel-a-day increase in September. The agency said the gains came from high Gulf exports despite continuing attacks on energy infrastructure and tankers.

Saudi Arabia produced 9.99 million barrels a day in October, up from 8.93 million in September, the IEA said. Kuwait produced 2.03 million barrels a day, up from 1.58 million.

The IEA's estimates differ from figures that Gulf producers submitted to OPEC. Iraq told OPEC it produced 4.888 million barrels a day in October, above the IEA's 4.6 million. Saudi Arabia told OPEC it pumped 10.563 million barrels a day, compared with the IEA's 9.99 million.

The recovery in Gulf output follows disruption from the U.S.-Israeli war on Iran, which began in late February.

Four ideal signings for Mauricio Pochettino’s Man Utd in January

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Mauricio Pochettino is set to take the reins at Manchester United and they’ll be hoping for an immediate boost in their fortunes – but they might have to add to their squad at the next opportunity to give themselves the best chance.

United’s season so far has been a disaster. They’ve gone into the international break sitting in the Premier League’s relegation zone. It’s cost Michael Carrick his job as manager, but quite rightly, the blame is more widespread.

United’s owners, directors and players have all come in for criticism, but if anything’s going to change, it’s probably the latter.

It’d be wrong to expect a mass overhaul in January, a notoriously difficult transfer window to do big business in, but it’d be equally daft to expect United to just sit around and do nothing.

A new manager coming in gives them a chance to bring in new ideas and players to suit those ideas.

So, keeping it semi-realistic, who’d be a good signing for them under Pochettino?

It’s tempting whenever a manager gets a new job to look at who’s at his former clubs that he might like to reunite with.

Ironically in this case, the one familiar player that might most appeal from Tottenham isn’t someone Pochettino worked with at Tottenham – it’s been a while, after all – but at Chelsea instead.

Gallagher was Pochettino’s most-used player during his sole season at Chelsea, playing in 48 of his 51 games in charge.

Sure, United already did a lot of work on their midfield over the summer, but they still seem to be missing runners there. And if there’s one thing Gallagher can do, it’s run. And if there’s one thing Pochettino wants his midfielders to do… you get the picture.

On average, Gallagher has covered 12.2km per 90 minutes when playing in the Premier League this season. It’s the highest average in the squad of anyone with three or more starts.

Pochettino used to rave about Gallagher at Chelsea. Exhibit A, this ringing endorsement in February 2024:

“He is a player with a great commitment to the team. He is always trying to compensate in every situation, in offensive and defensive situations. It’s priceless to have a player like him.

“‘He is in the starting XI nearly every game and one of the captains. For me, he is the type of player that the club need to have.

“He runs, he plays, he fights. He does everything to win. He is a really important player that the coaching staff want to have on the team.”

Gallagher hasn’t exactly been a regular starter for Spurs this season, but he only joined them in January, so he might be tricky to prise away.

But hypothetically, he’d get more of a look in from Pochettino in a midfield where Kobbie Mainoo is a top prospect, but Carlos Baleba has had injury issues, Youri Tielemans hasn’t convinced with his energy and Andrey Santos remains an unknown quantity.

Santos spent the 2023-24 season out on loan away from Chelsea rather than as part of Pochettino’s squad.

Gallagher would add to United’s homegrown quota too, but for a Pochettino team, he wouldn’t just be making up the numbers.

Pochettino got the best out of Luke Shaw when they worked together at Southampton, but Luke Shaw then was different to Luke Shaw now.

Back in those days he was a talented teenage prospect. Nowadays, he’s United’s tired only natural option at left-back. Quite simply, they need to freshen it up.

Pochettino will probably be happy to keep Shaw around as a senior, trustworthy figure. But he’ll be wanting more energy from the full-back positions and that means fresh legs.

Some of Pochettino’s best teams thrived when their full-backs bombed on to offer width while the wingers cut inside. Shaw can’t really do that anymore.

One player who can get up and down the flank is Palace’s Mitchell, who might not be the most inspiring choice but could be the best of what’s available.

Palace’s precarious position, coupled with the fact that Mitchell is out of contract at the end of the season, could make January his ideal time to escape.

His attacking output hasn’t always been cited as a strong point, but he can cover plenty of ground up and down the channel. A Pochettino team don’t necessarily need their full-backs to be the ones getting goals and assists, just to offer support and threatening runs.

Mitchell also has a good record in duels and could offer United more stability in defence, as they desperately need. There might be a signing with a higher ceiling to make, but that’s the kind of thing that might have to wait until the summer.

It’s not just at left-back United need to upgrade. The battle between Diogo Dalot and Noussair Mazraoui on the other side is hardly awe-inspiring, but unless they can shift one or the other, finding an upgrade might be a summer job.

At centre-back, though, they might not be able to afford to wait. Getting Harry Maguire back from injury will be a boost when the time comes, but with Lisandro Martinez suffering a sharp downturn in form, a brand new centre-back would help.

We reckon Nottingham Forest’s Murillo would be a pretty smart signing for United. He’s strong and imposing but also good on the ball, which is perfect for Pochettino.

Is he gettable in January, though? Probably not. He signed a new contract in the summer and we know what Forest are like when it comes to selling players. Or stopping them from being sold.

File this one under the dream signing category. Murillo has Premier League experience, plenty of good years still ahead of him and the attributes that Pochettino values highly in his defenders.

Look, we can safely say Pochettino would love to work with Harry Kane again. We know there have been numerous times when United would have loved to sign Harry Kane. It’s just not happening, though, is it?

Ink dry on his new Bayern Munich contract, Kane is off the menu. But a striker is still high on United’s wish list.

By January, Benjamin Sesko will be just about ready to return from injury, but who knows in what state. In his absence, United don’t have great alternatives. Joshua Zirkzee has had enough chances now and not taken them, while Matheus Cunha and Bryan Mbeumo are probably better out wide.

The summer transfer window is usually the more likely option for big striker moves. United could still do with someone to put them on, though.

The right kind of middle-ground between luxury signing and mere stop-gap could be someone like Borussia Dortmund’s Guirassy.

He’s the same height as Kane at 6’2 and boasts some similar strengths, such as his link-up play and chance conversion.

With eight goals from 14 games this season, including four from four in the Champions League, he’s in solid form. It’s no anomaly, looking at his last few seasons: 22 goals last time out, 38 the season before, 30 in the one before that.

At 30 years old, he’s not necessarily the long-term solution. But Kane is 33 and you’d sign him, wouldn’t you?

Guirassy might be one of the best options out there in terms of value for money.

Canada is threatening to sue Stellantis over Brampton plant sale

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

Industry Minister Mélanie Joly said Stellantis has 30 days to address a notice of default tied to a federal funding agreement

Canada warned it may take Stellantis $STLA to court over the automaker's plan to sell its shuttered factory in Brampton, Ontario, giving it 30 days to resolve Ottawa's objections. Industry Minister Mélanie Joly told a Toronto news conference on Thursday that Ottawa had formally notified Stellantis it is in default.

"We sent a notice of default and, basically, they have 30 days — or else, of course, we will sue them," Joly told reporters, according to CNBC.

Joly said Stellantis broke the contractual promises it made to Ottawa and to Brampton's workforce when it revealed it had struck a deal to sell the facility to Roshel, which makes armored vehicles. She said the company is "on the hook" for several hundred million dollars.

Stellantis, the parent of Jeep, Dodge, Fiat, Chrysler and Peugeot, announced on Monday a deal to sell the plant to Roshel, though it did not disclose the price. The company said it recognizes what it owes the facility's employees and is ready to negotiate a settlement for union-represented workers in talks with Unifor, Canada's largest private sector union.

Stellantis Canada CEO Trevor Longley said on Thursday that the automaker will give back C$24 million in subsidies it received from Ottawa, according to CNBC. That figure falls short of what the government estimates Stellantis owes.

"We recognize the disappointment and uncertainty surrounding the future of the facility. While our decision to sell the plant represents a change from the original plans for Brampton, the agreement we have advanced with a Canadian manufacturer provides a path to return the facility to productive industrial use," Longley said, according to CNBC.

A Stellantis spokesperson was not available for comment on Friday morning, according to CNBC.

Prime Minister Mark Carney has called the situation at the plant “unacceptable” and a “betrayal” of its workers, according to CNBC.

Stellantis had dropped its plan to build the Jeep Compass at Brampton, moving future output to an Illinois factory as U.S. auto tariffs and other pressures in the market weighed on the company, according to CNBC. The U.S. and Canada are in a trade dispute, and President Donald Trump has urged carmakers to shift their vehicle manufacturing to the United States.

Over the past 24 hours, crypto exchanges liquidated 187,389 traders for a total of $1.65 billion, and 88% of that amount came from long positions, according to CoinGlass. Bitcoin traders lost the most at $919.56 million, followed by Ethereum traders at $297.24 million.

Finally, Nvidia is expected to report its quarterly earnings on Nov. 17.

Nearly half of U.S. weight-loss drug users are changing how they take them, CDC finds

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

Skipping doses was the most common change at 28%, and 38% of users who made changes cited cost, according to the CDC

The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention released a report on Thursday showing that 44% of U.S. adults taking GLP-1 medications such as Wegovy and Zepbound changed their use of the drugs from the way they were prescribed.

Skipping doses topped the list of changes, with 28% of users saying they did it. Among users who made changes, cost was the reason for 38% and availability for 36%.

The data come from the CDC’s Rapid Surveys System, which collected responses from 8,118 U.S. adults in July and August 2025. Overall, an estimated 9.8% of adults said they had used a GLP-1 drug at some point over the past year. The 976 users in the sample received follow-up questions about their use.

Younger adults reported the most changes. Modification rates were 54% among users ages 18 to 39 and 30% among users 65 and older. Delayed prescription fills were reported by 37% of the younger group and 6% of the older group, and 58% of younger users who made changes cited cost, compared with 20% of older users.

Compounded formulations were used by 22.6% of GLP-1 users, and another 8.8% could not say if theirs was one. The Food and Drug Administration does not vet compounded GLP-1 medications for how safe, effective or well made they are before they go to market. Modification rates were 62% among compounded-drug users and 36% among other users.

About 12% of users reported reducing or stopping their medication because of side effects or symptoms. The CDC said more evidence on the safety and effectiveness of these real-world practices could help guide clinical guidelines.

The report noted limitations. The two survey panels had cumulative response rates of 13.3% and 3.8%, and the findings rely on self-reported answers.

The survey was conducted before Eli Lilly $LLY and Novo Nordisk intensified their push to make the drugs easier to get and to take on lower-priced compounded versions, according to Bloomberg. The federal government has also taken steps to broaden access, with Medicare patients among those covered.

Cost has been a barrier for some time. Without insurance or discounts, FDA-approved weight-loss drugs carry a sticker price that can top $1,000 a month, which helped drive demand for compounded versions during shortages of approved products.

The Medicare GLP-1 Bridge program, which began July 1, offers eligible beneficiaries weight-loss drugs for $50 a month.

The Midterms Are Over. History Says This Is What the Stock Market Does Next.

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Washington is divided again, and history has some encouraging data for investors.

Democrats won control of both the House and the Senate in the Nov. 3 midterm elections. That means President Trump will spend his final two years in office working with a Congress controlled by the other party. If you're wondering what this means for your portfolio, history offers some useful perspective.

Here's a statistic that sounds extremely reassuring. The S&P 500 has been higher 12 months after each of the 19 midterm elections since 1950, with an average gain of about 15%.

Even the weakest stretch, which included the 1987 crash, ended with a small gain. But the stock market has never been lower a year after a midterm election.

Five presidents since 1950 have served two full terms, and their final two years tell a mixed story. Here's how the S&P 500 did, including dividends:

One key takeaway is that the year after the midterm was positive all five times. The final year was more mixed, with two big declines. But it's worth noting why. The 2000 drop came as the dot-com bubble burst, and the 2008 drop was during the financial crisis. Neither had much to do with who controlled Congress.

Three of those presidents, Eisenhower, Reagan, and Bush, faced a Congress controlled by the other party in their final two years, just as Trump will. The results ranged from excellent to the 2008 crash. Over a longer period, RBC data shows that years with a Republican president and a Democratic Congress have averaged about a 5% annual gain, the lowest of any combination. Of course, that average is based on just a handful of periods, so I wouldn't read too much into it.

In short, history suggests that the year after a midterm tends to be good for stocks, and that a president's final stretch depends far more on the economy than on politics. Earnings, interest rates, and economic growth will drive the market over the next two years. My plan is the same as always. I'll stay invested and keep buying great businesses, no matter who controls Washington.

Once Upon a Farm is cutting its outlook after a toddler snack bar recall

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

The toddler snack maker lowered its full-year net sales forecast to $290 million-$300 million from $327 million-$335 million

Once Upon a Farm lowered its full-year 2026 outlook on Thursday to reflect the voluntary recall of its Tractor Wheels product lines. The company expects net sales of $290 million to $300 million, down from the $327 million to $335 million it had forecast.

The company expects an adjusted EBITDA loss of $4.5 million to $7 million, compared with prior guidance of adjusted EBITDA of $3 million to $4.5 million. Adjusted EBITDA is earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization, with additional adjustments. The company said the recall's effect on the fourth quarter drove the update.

For the third quarter, net sales rose 34.9% to $89.1 million from $66.0 million a year earlier. Net loss narrowed to $3.8 million from $11.3 million. Adjusted EBITDA was a loss of $1.0 million, compared with a profit of $0.9 million a year earlier.

Gross margin fell to 36.1% from 42.1%. The company cited trade investment for a national club program, product mix and cooler placement costs. As of Sept. 30, it held $106.4 million in cash and cash equivalents and no debt.

"We are committed to resolving the Tractor Wheels situation correctly, continuing to earn the trust of families and retailers, and executing against the significant growth opportunity ahead of us," CEO and co-founder John Foraker said in a statement.

Foraker told Bloomberg the company anticipates as much as $30 million in recall expenses, spanning written-off inventory, legal fees and product returns. For 2027, Foraker expects the company to reach breakeven or better on an adjusted EBITDA basis, with net sales no lower than $330 million, according to Bloomberg.

The company plans to change the bars' recipe and packaging before bringing them back to store shelves, Foraker told Bloomberg. It has brought in outside specialists in food safety to review the product, according to Bloomberg.

Once Upon a Farm announced the voluntary recall of its Tractor Wheels soft-baked bars on Oct. 30, citing three consumer complaints about a choking hazard. The bars are labeled for toddlers ages 1.5 to 2.5, and the recall covers all lots across three varieties.

Tractor Wheels accounted for about 18% of the company's revenue this year as of the earlier coverage. Once Upon a Farm stock has lost over 40% of its value since it began trading in February.

$1,000 in Main Street Capital's 2007 IPO Is Worth About $7,300 Counting Dividends. It Has Never Cut Its Monthly Payout.

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Main Street Capital is one of the best, most consistent dividend stocks out there.

Investors looking for a good dividend-income stock may not find too many better than Main Street Capital (MAIN -0.78%).

The business development company (BDC) has raised its dividend for 19 straight years, every year since it went public in 2007 -- and has never once reduced its dividend.

And it has already raised its dividend for the first three months of 2027, putting it on track for 20 straight years of dividend raises.

Main Street Capital is also one of the relatively few dividend stocks that pay a monthly dividend, so the income stream comes more frequently than most.

BDCs, like real estate investment trusts (REITs), are required by law to pay out 90% of their taxable income in dividends in exchange for getting certain tax advantages. So they are literally built to generate dividend income.

Main Street Capital went public in 2007 as a BDC that focuses on providing debt and equity capital solutions for mostly smaller companies that can't get funding from traditional commercial banks or private equity partners.

But what makes it different, and superior to most, is that its portfolio is internally managed, as opposed to being managed by a third party, so it has lower fees because it doesn't pay fees to a third party. But it also takes an ownership stake in its companies, in addition to its loans, so it is more invested in companies' success and can directly benefit from their growth.

That has helped it steadily feed its dividend for 19 years. It currently pays out a monthly dividend of $0.265 through the end of 2026, up from $0.255 in the same period a year ago. Main Street Capital currently pays out a high yield of about 7.68%.

On Nov. 3, Main Street approved a $0.27 monthly dividend for the first three months of 2027 -- marking a 3.8% increase over the first quarter of 2026. This would be the 20th straight year of dividend increases for Main Street Capital. It also authorized an additional $0.30 per share supplemental dividend for December 2026 because of excess taxable income.

Main Street Capital went public in October 2007 at roughly $15 per share. If you invested $1,000 in the stock at its IPO, that would have bought you roughly 67 shares. At its current share price of around $57 per share, that $1,000 would be worth about $3,800 today.

But then how much dividend income would you have accumulated?

Calculating all dividends declared to date, including the first-quarter 2027 dividends and the supplemental December 2026 dividend, Main Street will have paid $52.315 per share in cash dividends since its October 2007 IPO. If you multiply $52.315 times the 67 shares, you would have pocketed about $3,500 in dividend income, alongside the approximately 7.2% average annualized return you made on Main Street's stock since its IPO.

So, if you combined the dividend income with the capital appreciation, that $1,000 investment in 2007 would be worth about $7,300 today.

Inside the $3.25 Million Pasadena Glass Pavilion That’s Still Studied in Architecture Classes Today

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

A three-bedroom midcentury modern home designed by Case Study architect Craig Ellwood has just come on the market in Pasadena, CA, for $3.25 million. It features a notable cantilevered glass pavilion.

Built in 1965, the Kubly House was originally constructed for less than $45,000. The 2,227-square-foot home has since been thoughtfully updated.

“What makes this house special is its architecture and design, and how true it has remained to the original,” says Chris Suarez, who is co-listing the property with Kimberly Benz of Suarez + Benz at Compass. “Nothing was significantly altered. Everything done was intended to restore the house to its original condition. The footprint, the floors, the glass—everything has remained true to the original design.”

Located in the Linda Vista neighborhood, the home has an iconic redwood, cantilevered glass pavilion that opens to a single-level floor plan. The private half-acre lot includes a pool and spa surrounded by lush landscaping with eucalyptus and oak trees.

With exposed post-and-beam construction, clean horizontal lines, and expansive walls of glass, the home was designed to give the illusion of floating above its natural surroundings. The deck was recently restored using specially milled wood.

“It was sourced from northern Washington and brought down here,” Suarez notes. “Everything was done painstakingly to avoid altering the house’s integrity, architecture, and design. That being said, significant work has been done, including updates to the roof, infrastructure, air conditioning, and electrical systems. All the major systems have been updated, but you wouldn’t necessarily know it because the work was done so carefully.”

Originally commissioned by ArtCenter College of Design alumni and educators Don and Sally Kubly, the home represents their understanding of design and the culture of the college community. It is still studied in classes there.

“Don was ArtCenter’s first president in Pasadena,” Suarez explains. “There’s also a connection through Craig Ellwood, who designed ArtCenter’s main campus building. The main campus is an incredible steel-and-glass building designed by Ellwood. Don played a significant role in moving the school from Los Angeles to Pasadena, where he served as president for many years. He’s very well known within that community, and this house is significant to the college’s history and culture.”

Based on available architectural records, the Kubly House is said to be Ellwood’s only documented residential project in Pasadena.

After remaining in the Kubly family for nearly six decades, the home changed hands for $3.6 million in 2022. Since then, the sellers have undertaken a preservation-minded restoration with Carl Bronson of Bronstruction, updating major systems to contemporary standards.

“A house that helped usher in much midcentury modernism was the Glass House, designed by Philip Johnson around 1948 or 1949,” Benz notes. “That design was definitely something Ellwood loved. You can clearly see that house’s influence here, which is important because it reveals some of the influences behind Ellwood’s work.”

Benz adds that the home’s design was also influenced by Ellwood’s peers, including midcentury designer George Nelson.

“A furniture plan developed for the house clearly referenced Mies van der Rohe and George Nelson, both important figures in midcentury modern design,” she says. “Even the deck and the steps leading to the house resemble a George Nelson bench. There’s actually a bench built into the concrete wall near the pool that resembles a George Nelson design. I don’t know whether Nelson designed or made it, but the influence is certainly there.”

The house is special because many of the original details have remained intact, she adds.

“You can see the influence of the people Ellwood admired throughout the house,” Benz says. “That significance is what makes this house important when you look at the midcentury modernist movement. All these examples are visible in the house and remain there.”

Some of the built-in furniture has a distinctive style, too.

“There’s a stunning floating credenza that looks like a George Nelson piece,” she notes. “We think it might be one of his designs. It’s in the living room. There’s also a built-in console that might be a George Nelson design as well. So, some of the furniture is actually built into the architecture.”

While some pieces are originals and others are reproductions, Benz says, “the most important thing was following the original plan. The history of this house is everything.”

When staging the home, she says they were “fortunate to discover an original interior design study among the blueprints, plans, and documents that came with the house. We were able to look at that study, and it became a guide and a road map for staging the property. Sourcing the furniture was a challenge because we had to find the specific pieces.”

The city of Pasadena has also identified the property as eligible for individual listing on the National Register of Historic Places.

As a writer and editor, she has worked for a variety of online and print publications, including Inked magazine, Jetset, Westchester magazine, JustLuxe, The Boston Globe, and U.S. Veterans Magazine.
