Detroit’s innovation lesson: what kids hear about careers vs. what they actually do

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Anika Goss is president and CEO of Detroit Future City.

Angelique Power is president and CEO of The Skillman Foundation. Their organizations co-produced Career-Connected Learning: Investing in Detroit’s Future Talent (May 2026), the report cited in this piece; Skillman funded the research.

Some of America’s most influential leaders will soon gather in Detroit for the Fortune 500 Innovation Forum to discuss innovation, the pace of change and what it takes to compete. In classrooms across the same city, the workers their companies will need a decade from now are hearing about careers they may never get to try.

When Detroit Future City and The Skillman Foundation surveyed 83 young Detroiters in 2025 about career exposure, 78 percent said they had heard from a guest speaker or professional mentor. Fewer than half had done an internship or paid work experience. Just 35 percent had received hands-on training.

Detroit’s teenagers are not short on talk about work. They are short on chances to do it.

Jordan, a composite of students in our focus groups, shows what that costs. At 14, she loved sketching buildings, rooms and imagined cities. But no one suggested her interests might point toward a career in architecture or design. She discovered architecture years later, by accident, and realized there were pathways she could have explored in middle school.

Her story is common. A student who loved performing did not learn about physical therapy until nearly graduating. A true crime enthusiast had no idea criminology was a career. Students in skilled trades programs wished they had discovered those opportunities earlier.

Some youth are best suited for college, while others may pursue careers directly after high school. Both need opportunities to explore options and find a path forward.

Across the country, employers report trouble finding the right skills and experience. In a 2025 U.S. Chamber of Commerce survey, just 38 percent of hiring managers said finding candidates with the right skills was easy; 37 percent said the same about experience. The report, produced with the College Board, also found that 84 percent believe most high school graduates are not workforce-ready.

That points to a blind spot. Workforce programs focus on the late stages: choosing a major, enrolling in training, applying for a job. By then, young people like Jordan have already formed assumptions about which careers exist and where they belong.

Our May 2026 report, which Skillman funded, started with young people themselves: surveys and focus groups with nearly 200 Detroit youth, along with conversations with educators, employers and community leaders.

What we heard was consistent: Young people want earlier career exposure, mentors to explain different pathways and opportunities to connect school with the world beyond it. They are not asking adults to decide their futures. They want the possibilities to be more visible and accessible.

That is not just a Detroit challenge. According to NAF’s 2025–2026 Student Survey, 47 percent of students who did not participate in work-based learning cited a lack of awareness of available opportunities. Meanwhile, 79 percent of students who participated said those experiences helped them identify and develop skills for their future careers.

Exposure alone is not enough. In Detroit, talk is plentiful and practice is scarce.

Businesses can build on Career and Technical Education programs by offering workplace exposure, mentors, internships, apprenticeships and other forms of work-based learning. These experiences help students test their interests, build skills and understand career requirements.

Apprenticeships show the payoff. According to the U.S. Department of Labor, more than 800,000 people participate in apprenticeship programs annually nationwide. And the department’s April 2026 ApprenticeshipUSA fact sheet reports that 93 percent of apprentices who complete their programs retain employment, demonstrating the value of connecting education with hands-on experience and career opportunities.

Business cannot do this alone; educators, workforce organizations, higher education, philanthropy and community partners all have roles. The goal is to help young people see their options, not steer them toward one path.

Detroit Public Schools Community District’s High School Redesign strengthens connections between education, career exploration and life after graduation. Beginning in the 2026–27 school year, it expands advising, academic supports and college- and career-connected experiences across neighborhood high schools.

Henry Ford Health’s RYSE MED program offers another example, providing hands-on opportunities to explore careers in medicine and health care. From 2022 to 2026, the program enrolled 120 high school students, including 45 who attended DPSCD schools.

If businesses want stronger pipelines tomorrow, they cannot wait until students graduate to begin building them. They need to become visible earlier, in classrooms, in after-school programs, in summer internships, in middle schools, through meaningful opportunities for young people to learn by doing.

Detroit’s executives will spend the Forum talking about innovation. The students in the city around them are already hearing plenty. What they need is the chance to try the work, and business can give it to them.

Names and some identifying details have been changed to protect the privacy of youth research participants. Jordan is a composite based on real focus group accounts from the authors’ research and does not depict any one participant’s account verbatim.

The opinions expressed in Fortune.com commentary pieces are solely the views of their authors and do not necessarily reflect the opinions and beliefs of Fortune.

13 Fab Old Photos of 2026 Rock & Roll Hall of Fame Inductee Sade Adu

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Think Sade, and a few things likely pop to mind. There’s the signature beauty look: the dark eyeliner, shapely brows, red lip, and long, slicked-back plait. There are the clothes: good earrings—often big gold hoops—and stage outfits that were sexy, yes, but also slyly demure; high-waisted trousers, semi-sheer turtlenecks, cropped jackets, qipao-style dresses. You may nerdily make the distinction between Sade the woman—Helen Folasade Adu, OBE, born on January 16, 1959, in Ibadan, Nigeria—and Sade the Grammy-winning band, formed in 1982 and made up of Adu, Paul Denman, Andrew Hale, and Stuart Matthewman.

Now there’s one more thing to add to that list: the Rock & Roll Hall of Fame. This weekend, the band will celebrate their induction—along with acts including Phil Collins, Billy Idol, Oasis, and the late Luther Vandross—in a ceremony at Los Angeles’s Peacock Theater. Now, will any of Sade’s members actually be present for that ceremony? No, according to a statement released on November 10. Even so, they called the recognition “both a gift and a timely reminder to give thanks, not only to those who voted for us but also every single person who lets our music into their lives.”

And what music that is: from “Smooth Operator” to “Kiss of Life,” “Is It a Crime” to “No Ordinary Love,” “The Sweetest Taboo” to “I Couldn’t Love You More,” Sade’s soulful, sui generis sound remains a benchmark for timeless cool.

Here, in celebration of her group’s latest honor, we’ve rounded up 13 striking old photographs of the divine Sade Adu, a generational talent and forever style icon.

Sporting spotty shirting, high-waisted trousers, and a wide belt during a 1984 set in Rotterdam.

This is no ordinary love–and those are no ordinary neutrals. A graduate of London’s Saint Martin’s School of Art, Sade shows off her style bonafides at the Hammersmith Odeon in 1984.

Cooler than cool in sunglasses and a simple white button-down.

Performing at the Live Aid charity concert at Wembley Stadium in 1985.

Gold jewelry, jaunty bolero jacket, can’t lose.

In textured whites—and that classic red lip—at the Ambassador East Hotel in Chicago, 1985.

Making her Saturday Night Live debut on December 14, 1985, with Tom Hanks as host. The band performed “Is It a Crime” and “The Sweetest Taboo,” both from their second studio album, Promise.

In 2010, recalling the release of Sade’s debut album, Diamond Life, in 1984, The New Yorker’s Sasha Frere-Jones poetically described Adu’s voice on as “a grainy contralto full of air that betrays a slight ache but no agony, and values even imperfect dignity over a show of pain.”

Center-stage at at Northrop Auditorium in Minneapolis, 1993.

With the English singer-songwriter Leo Sayer at a charity ball in London, 2001.

Having a moment at the United Center in Chicago, 2001.

Red all over, in concert at PNC Bank Arts Center in Holmdel, New Jersey, 2001.

Trump says he wants to ‘get Cuba done’ before leaving office, would consider military force

📅 2026-11-13  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

U.S. President Donald Trump has said he wants to "get Cuba done" before leaving office in 2029, adding that he would "certainly consider" military intervention against the communist-run Caribbean island.

In an interview with conservative radio host Hugh Hewitt on Thursday, Trump said his administration was monitoring the situation in Havana "very closely" and described Cuba as a "failed" state.

"I want to get Cuba done before I leave," Trump said. His comments marked the first time he has put a timeline on possible military intervention to topple Cuban authorities, according to Politico.

Trump said Secretary of State Marco Rubio, whose parents are from Cuba, "would love to do it" and said he would "certainly consider" a short military operation.

It comes as the U.S. continues to enforce a comprehensive economic embargo against Cuba, a campaign which has contributed to a deepening energy and economic crisis on the island. Cuba has suffered repeated nationwide blackouts in recent months, as well as fuel and food shortages.

The Cuban Embassy in Washington, D.C., estimated on Thursday that the cost of the U.S. blockade to Cuba between March 2025 and February 2026 amounted to $8 billion.

Earlier this month, the Treasury Department imposed further sanctions on Cuban oil tankers and shipping companies and threatened to target any international entity or person doing business within its marine or maritime industries as part of a push "to end the Cuban regime's malign activities, both in Cuba and across our hemisphere."

In response, Cuba's Embassy in Washington, D.C., said these steps "deepen an energy siege that already leaves hospitals without reliable power and families facing blackouts. The blockade is not targeted at a government. It is collective punishment that hits ordinary people every day."

Trump has previously talked up the prospect of a "friendly takeover" of Havana and said the White House could turn its sights on Cuba after the Iran war. The U.S. president has also said he could do anything he wanted with the country, adding that he thinks he will have the "honor" of "taking Cuba."

In May, Cuban Energy Minister Vicente de la O Levy said the country of roughly 10 million had run out of fuel oil and diesel, describing the country's predicament as "extremely tense."

Analysts have typically been skeptical about the prospect of a U.S. military campaign against Cuba, suggesting that further economic pressure against the island's authorities appears more likely.

Former NVIDIA CEO Jensen Huang described Collette Kress, the company’s CFO who has now replaced him as interim CEO, as “a deer in the headlights” during a presentation earlier in the summer, the Wall Street Journal reported, citing an anonymous source.

Coinbase to Delist 12 Crypto Trading Pairs, ADA-ETH and XLM-BTC Among Them

📅 2026-11-13  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Disclaimer: The opinions expressed by our writers are their own and do not represent the views of U.Today. The financial and market information provided on U.Today is intended for informational purposes only. U.Today is not liable for any financial losses incurred while trading cryptocurrencies. Conduct your own research by contacting financial experts before making any investment decisions. We believe that all content is accurate as of the date of publication, but certain offers mentioned may no longer be available.

Major crypto exchange Coinbase is set to suspend trading in 12 crypto trading pairs as part of an effort to improve overall market health and consolidate liquidity on the platform. This decision affects two trading pairs involving Cardano (ADA) and Stellar (XLM): ADA/ETH and XLM/BTC.

Coinbase said it regularly monitors the markets on its exchange and, in line with this, trading will be suspended on the named markets on November 25, 2026. In an effort to improve overall market health and consolidate liquidity, Coinbase will be removing a number of non-USD trading pairs for supported assets.

According to an announcement, Coinbase will suspend trading on the following markets on November 25, 2026, at or around 12 PM ET: APE-USDT, ATOM-USDT, ALGO-BTC, CRV-BTC, ETC-BTC, ICP-BTC, XLM-BTC, APE-EUR, ADA-ETH, 1INCH-GBP, CHZ-GBP, SNX-GBP.

However, Coinbase Advanced Trade users in eligible regions can still trade these assets in the USD order books.

In light of this, APE-USDT, ATOM-USDT, ALGO-BTC, CRV-BTC, ETC-BTC, ICP-BTC, XLM-BTC, APE-EUR, ADA-ETH, 1INCH-GBP, CHZ-GBP have been moved to limit-only mode on Coinbase Exchange and Coinbase Advanced.

In recent delistings, Coinbase has disabled trading for Balancer (BAL) and Tensor (TNSR), but users will continue to have the ability to withdraw their funds at any time.

The suspension of trading for Stargate Finance (STG) and Vulcan Forged (PYR) has also been postponed to November 20, 2026, at or around 2 PM ET. Based on recent reviews, Coinbase will suspend trading for Ampleforth Governance Token (FORTH) on November 23, 2026, at or around 2 PM ET. Trading for Neon EVM (NEON) and Syndicate (SYND) will be suspended on November 16, 2026, at or around 2 PM ET.

Spot trading for Pearl (PEARL) will go live on November 13, 2026. The opening of the PEARL-USD trading pair will begin at or after 9 AM PT, if liquidity conditions are met, in regions where trading is supported.

Perpetual futures trading for GRAM, GRASS, QNT and ZRO will go live on November 13, 2026, at or after 12:00 PM UTC.

Cemig and Huawei Jointly Deploy 450 MHz Private Wireless Network

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

JOHANNESBURG, Nov. 13, 2026 /PRNewswire/ -- As distribution network services grew increasingly complex, Cemig's distribution devices relied primarily on leased public networks for data backhaul. However, this model presented significant risks.

To address these challenges, Cemig conducted multiple rounds of technical evaluation and global use case research, ultimately selecting Huawei as its strategic partner to jointly deploy the first commercial 450 MHz private wireless network for the power sector in Latin America.

After field testing, the customer gained firsthand experience of the benefits delivered by the private wireless network, strengthening their confidence in distribution grid modernization and raising high expectations for the future energy transition: "Huawei submitted the best proposal during the bidding process, offering a solution that not only meets our current distribution grid modernization requirements but also scales for future renewable energy integration. Huawei has extensive experience in the energy industry. With Huawei's private network solution, Cemig will secure a high-quality private communications network. For Cemig, this partnership goes far beyond technology; it is crucial to our future business growth."

1. Improved power supply reliabilityThe private wireless network lifted distribution terminal connectivity to 99% and communication reliability to 99.99%, significantly improving Cemig's power distribution service levels.

2. Mitigated business risksThe private wireless network can significantly improve line losses, SAIDI, and SAIFI, thereby helping Cemig avoid regulatory penalties.

3. Assured investment returnsThe private wireless network greatly enhances power services, ensuring Cemig meets ANEEL's regulatory requirements and secures favorable investment returns.

4. Expanded core production applicationsAs the foundational communications backbone for power production operations, the private wireless network delivers reliable last-mile connectivity for today's distribution automation. Furthermore, it supports future core applications, such as massive smart meter integration, rapid distributed PV adoption, and complex microgrid management, driving the transition from traditional load-based power generation to dynamic interaction across source, grid, load, and storage.

For more details, please visit: https://e.huawei.com/en/case-studies/industries/grid/202611-cemig-450-mhz

Official: RedBird launch ‘Milan Sports & Entertainment’ company – the initial focuses

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Today, RedBird Capital Partners have announced the creation of a new company that will focus specifically on AC Milan.

This season, Gerry Cardinale’s commitments to Milan have been seen much more. The American has been at every game in Serie A, proving his desire to be closer to the team and it feels like his relationship with the club (and fans) is growing deeper.

Read SempreMilan ad-free and get access to exclusive news. Click here for a free trial!

He’s still got room to learn, but it’s a substantial improvement from recent years and now, he’s taken a new step forward.

As MilanNews relays, RedBird Capital Partners have now launched a new holding company for anything and everything related to the Rossoneri, calling ‘Milan Sports & Entertainment’, MSE for short.

This company will ‘consolidate RedBird’s Milan-based investments in sports, infrastructure, and entertainment’ and organise individual assets into a ‘multi-sport platform’.

The report continues, stating that MSE will help to further transform Milano into one of the ‘most influential sports and entertainment markets in the world’ by combining its importance in fashion, design, culture, sports and entertainment.

Above all, though, it’s another representation of RedBird’s long-term investment and commitment to the club (and the city).

San Donato is also referenced, with MSE making a commitment to the area, and there are plans to create a stadium and arena management business, which could eventually culminate in the Rossoneri’s NBA team.

Finally, RedBird have formed a new leadership team for the group, with Brett Yormark appointed the CEO of the company. He will report directly to Cardinale and will lead the ‘development and implementation of MSE’s strategy, overseeing current portfolio businesses and evaluating future investment opportunities.’

Angus Crichton Promoted to Head of Global Product at Scenic

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Scenic Group has promoted Angus Crichton to head of global product, bringing his experience across sales, marketing and product development to the leadership of its product teams for Scenic and Emerald Cruises & Tours.

Now based at the Group’s office in Zug, Switzerland, he will lead product development across luxury river cruising, yacht cruising and escorted land journeys, strengthening the connection between itinerary design and the guest experience.

“As the only company to design, build, own and operate our ships, we have a truly unique ability to innovate across every aspect of the guest experience, from the luxury spaces on board to the destinations we visit and the journeys we create,” said Sture Myrmell, chief operating officer, Scenic Group.

“That same philosophy connects our ocean, river and land journeys, giving Scenic and Emerald Cruises & Tours guests the confidence to explore in different ways while enjoying a seamless experience with the brands they know and trust. Angus’s deep understanding of our business makes him ideally placed to bring these strengths together as we expand our global portfolio.”

Crichton previously led marketing, sales and product for Emerald in Australia and New Zealand, alongside responsibility for the land product division across Scenic and Emerald.

“Having been part of Scenic Group’s evolution over more than 26 years, I’m excited to help drive its next chapter. Bringing product development and our commercial teams closer together will help us deliver exceptional journeys for our guests and give our travel partners compelling experiences to sell,” noted Crichton.

The appointment comes as Scenic Group progresses what it says is its most significant expansion program in decades, with eight new ships planned for 2027 and 2028 and further announcements to come across its luxury river and yacht fleets.

River fleet additions include the Emerald Lumi on France’s Seine River. The ship recently touched water for the first time.

Scenic is also planning expansion to India’s Hooghly River in 2027, with a further ship planned for India’s Brahmaputra River in 2028.

The Scenic Spirit II and Scenic Spirit III are set to debut on the Mekong River from 2028.

The three new luxury yachts comprise the Emerald Raiya in 2027, followed by the Emerald Xara and the Scenic Ikon in 2028.

The Ikon will debut in the Mediterranean in April 2028, with Antarctica featuring in its inaugural season.

New York Times Blames Trump For Air Safety Risks—Gets Blasted By The Independent NTSB

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The National Transportation Safety Board says the New York Times distorted its comments to attack President Trump. The paper’s aviation safety story has bigger problems than just politics. It gets a major crash wrong and credits a pilot hours rule for a safety record that began years before the rule took effect. James Fallows’s “Is Air Travel Getting Less Safe?” describes real issues of overworked air traffic controllers, military aircraft operating around passenger flights, and safety recommendations that haven’t been adequately addressed. Then it tries to turn them into a story about Trump dismantling the institutions that keep us safe, even though the issues he cites have been going on far longer than the current administration. I’ve written predominantly critically about Trump since he first ran for president 11 years ago. I rejected his effort to blame diversity hiring for the American Airlines 5342 tragedy before investigators had established what happened. That doesn’t make every argument against him a good one. The Times introduces Fallows as an instrument-rated private pilot with more than 2,000 hours. That’s true but while he’s written extensively about aviation it’s an odd way to frame him as author of this story. He’s also Jimmy Carter’s former chief speechwriter, a longtime Atlantic writer and editor, and a familiar voice on NPR. His career has been about politics and public policy. His own introduction to the article frames the piece as meant to connect aviation under Trump with public health, international aid, diplomacy, higher education and the rule of law. Aviation is the vehicle for a larger political point. The piece isn’t really about aviation, it’s using aviation. The Independent NTSB Attacks The Piece The NTSB says the article: Distorts the agency’s responses with political statements intended to attack the current administration. Mischaracterizes the NTSB’s role in the 1,500 hour pilot requirement. Incorrectly implies negligence by the Black Hawk pilots involved in the Washington National Airport collision. The statement also thanks the administration for supporting the agency and providing resources. That last part is political messaging too. (Gratitude for a budget doesn’t answer address aviation safety, but is probably necessary as a defensive measure.) “We are disappointed that this article sensationalizes and politicizes tragedies.” The NTSB’s response to the New York Times Magazine. — NTSB Newsroom (@NTSB_Newsroom) November 12, 2026 Fallows interviewed NTSB Chair Jennifer Homendy. The agency argues she’s being mischaracterized, but they don’t actually say how. In the article she says, “Recommendations alone don’t save lives. Action does.” Her own July statement about the Washington crash warned that delays implementing the board’s recommendations represented a continuing, unacceptable risk. The Article Gets The Colgan Air Crash Wrong Fallows spends considerable space describing the 2009 Colgan Air crash near Buffalo – as the last U.S. airline crash with loss of life before American 5342. In fact, PenAir flight 3296 overran the runway at Unalaska in October 2019, killing a passenger. His account has the Colgan Air plane accumulating ice until it becomes a “frigid brick.” He writes that by the time the captain recognized the problem, it “might already have become unflyable.” But that isn’t what the investigation found. The FAA’s accident analysis says icing wasn’t a cause. When the stall warning activated, the airplane wasn’t yet stalled. The captain pulled back on the controls and put it into a stall. The crew had failed to monitor their airspeed and mishandled the approach. And both Colgan pilots already had more than 1,500 hours: The captain had 3,379 hours. The captain had a history of training failures despite the hours. The first officer had 2,244 hours, including 1,470 hours as a light airplane flight instructor and 774 hours in turbine aircraft. The 1,500 Hour Rule For Co-Pilots Isn’t The Reason For The Safety Improvements After Colgan, Congress and regulators made several changes. Pilot rest rules improved. Training changed. Pilot qualification requirements changed. Sharing pilot records improved. Evidence that safety has performed well after a package of changes doesn’t establish that (1) every element of those changes (2) caused the improvements. And there’s strong reason to believe the the 1,500 hour rule has the opposite effect. It makes becoming a pilot take far longer and become far more costly, which keeps talented people from becoming pilots. Those with a higher opportunity cost, or lacking access to resources needed, get excluded from the pool of potential pilots. You’re then drawing from a smaller group. The NTSB didn’t recommend the 1,500 hour number. In 2010 Senate testimony, then-chair Deborah Hersman explained that the board had investigated accidents involving both high-time and low-time pilots. It found a relationship between failed practical tests and subsequent accidents, but there wasn’t evidence that number of hours actually mattered. The higher minimum took effect in 2013. Fallows’s safety streak begins in February 2009. The first four and a half years of that streak can’t be credited to a requirement that wasn’t yet in effect. Making pilots chase hours can allow relevant skills to deteriorate and bad habits to develop before an airline has to train those habits out of them. Repeated flights in clear weather, making takeoffs and landings at the same familiar airports, aren’t equivalent to structured training for instrument flying in a jet with another pilot, airline procedures, and complex air traffic control instructions. Hours in a hot air balloon count. The hot air balloon can be tethered. European co-pilots don’t face the same restriction, but their safety record is equivalent. Sub-1,500 hour European pilots fly in the United States every day without concern (and American pilots fly in Europe without elevated risk). The argument for the rule is that experience can build judgment, and that the higher threshold was part of a successful improvement in regional airline standards. But that’s an argument for useful experience and rigorous standards. That’s not what we got. An hour spent building time isn’t as valuable as an hour spent in a supervised airline training program. And there’s zero evidence that 1,500 is the right metric. It was chosen because it’s expensive and time-consuming, restricts entry into the pilot profession and drives up wages, and was supported by the major pilots union. The Black Hawk Crew Bears Responsibility The NTSB objected to Fallows implying negligence by the helicopter pilots. But its own probable cause finding identifies the helicopter crew’s failure to maintain effective visual separation as a cause of the collision. The crew accepted responsibility for avoiding the airliner and failed to do so. The existence of systemic failures don’t eliminate pilot error. In Homendy’s testimony explaining the findings, the board concluded that the helicopter’s altimeter probably understated its altitude by about 100 feet. The crew likely believed it was below the route’s ceiling. Fallows also describes the crew flying through civilian traffic without monitoring its traffic avoidance systems. The helicopter had no integrated cockpit traffic display. The pilots had tablets capable of showing traffic, but looking down at them while flying low at night with night vision goggles was problematic. Their audio alerts weren’t integrated into the pilots’ helmets. There were failures of FAA route design, and the FAA failed to act in the face of repeated warnings. But it’s genuinely weird to pin the blame for that on the first nine days of the Trump administration before the crash. I’ve criticized efforts to preserve military exceptions around Washington National. Civilian aviation shouldn’t have to accommodate unnecessary military risks. The Pentagon needs to change, and the current administration is responsible for making that happen. But it didn’t invent the culture that allowed these risks to exist in the first place. The Article’s Own Incident Data Doesn’t Support A Partisan Cause Fallows combines airline accidents, rocket debris, military encounters, airshow incidents and presidential flyovers which generally have different causes. And a list that attributes incidents while a given President is in office to that President doesn’t do a good job of evaluating what decisions caused those incidents. But more simply, even the article’s own figures for the most serious runway incursions are problematic for the Fallows narrative: 2023: 22 incidents. 2024: 7 incidents. 2025: 14 incidents. 2026 through October: 9 incidents. The increase from 2024 to 2025 looks dramatic – a doubling! But it’s still far lower than under Biden in 2023! These are relatively small numbers, and they don’t show a steadily worsening trend. (And there are more aircraft operations than in 2023.) Air Traffic Control Was Broken Before Trump Fallows points to deregulation and falling fares as sources of overcrowding. More people being able to afford to fly is a good thing, and flying is still safer than driving. The failure is an FAA air traffic organization system that hasn’t kept up with demand. The Times has done excellent reporting on this subject. In August 2023, I covered its investigation into 46 close calls in one month and roughly 300 near collisions in a year. Staffing shortages, fatigue, outdated equipment and a culture that tolerated recurring warnings weren’t the result of the 2024 election. Fallows discusses 132 union-represented FAA employees fired during the DOGE purge. Their union announced their reinstatement in March 2025. Firing them was disruptive, but he leaves out the reversal. And recruiting gamers doesn’t establish that the FAA is lowering controller standards. The FAA has been growing the top of the funnel but there’s no indication that unqualified people are being advanced in training to a greater degree. I’ve criticized Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy for counting initial trainee hires as progress, that strikes me as juking the stats but hasn’t been a safety issue. And the Trump administration is doing modernization work that’s valuable and that’s been ignored for decades even if I’m skeptical that it’s close to enough because they don’t seem to be addressing the management problems at the FAA that created failed technology investment and decades-long controller shortages in the first place. I’ve been critical of pouring money into the existing organization. We need to address how air traffic control is managed, how it buys equipment, how it finances long projects and how it’s held accountable for performance. We’d be far better off separating the air traffic control operator from its regulator, with stable funding and professional management, as Canada has done with its nonprofit provider. (HT: Paddle Your Own Kanoo) Topics on this pageAviation safetyDonald TrumpJames FallowsNational Transportation Safety BoardThe New York TimesAir traffic controlAmerican AirlinesBuffaloCanadaColgan AirColgan Air Flight 3407Federal Aviation AdministrationJimmy CarterJoe BidenNational Public RadioRonald Reagan Washington National AirportSean DuffyThe TimesUnited States CongressUnited States Senate+15 more More From View from the Wing

Sub-1,500 hour European pilots fly in the United States every day without concern (and American pilots fly in Europe without elevated risk). The argument for the rule is that experience can build judgment, and that the higher threshold was part of a successful improvement in regional airline standards. But that’s an argument for useful experience and rigorous standards. That’s not what we got. An hour spent building time isn’t as valuable as an hour spent in a supervised airline training program. And there’s zero evidence that 1,500 is the right metric. It was chosen because it’s expensive and time-consuming, restricts entry into the pilot profession and drives up wages, and was supported by the major pilots union. The Black Hawk Crew Bears Responsibility The NTSB objected to Fallows implying negligence by the helicopter pilots. But its own probable cause finding identifies the helicopter crew’s failure to maintain effective visual separation as a cause of the collision. The crew accepted responsibility for avoiding the airliner and failed to do so. The existence of systemic failures don’t eliminate pilot error. In Homendy’s testimony explaining the findings, the board concluded that the helicopter’s altimeter probably understated its altitude by about 100 feet. The crew likely believed it was below the route’s ceiling. Fallows also describes the crew flying through civilian traffic without monitoring its traffic avoidance systems. The helicopter had no integrated cockpit traffic display. The pilots had tablets capable of showing traffic, but looking down at them while flying low at night with night vision goggles was problematic. Their audio alerts weren’t integrated into the pilots’ helmets. There were failures of FAA route design, and the FAA failed to act in the face of repeated warnings. But it’s genuinely weird to pin the blame for that on the first nine days of the Trump administration before the crash. I’ve criticized efforts to preserve military exceptions around Washington National. Civilian aviation shouldn’t have to accommodate unnecessary military risks. The Pentagon needs to change, and the current administration is responsible for making that happen. But it didn’t invent the culture that allowed these risks to exist in the first place. The Article’s Own Incident Data Doesn’t Support A Partisan Cause Fallows combines airline accidents, rocket debris, military encounters, airshow incidents and presidential flyovers which generally have different causes. And a list that attributes incidents while a given President is in office to that President doesn’t do a good job of evaluating what decisions caused those incidents. But more simply, even the article’s own figures for the most serious runway incursions are problematic for the Fallows narrative: 2023: 22 incidents. 2024: 7 incidents. 2025: 14 incidents. 2026 through October: 9 incidents. The increase from 2024 to 2025 looks dramatic – a doubling! But it’s still far lower than under Biden in 2023! These are relatively small numbers, and they don’t show a steadily worsening trend. (And there are more aircraft operations than in 2023.) Air Traffic Control Was Broken Before Trump Fallows points to deregulation and falling fares as sources of overcrowding. More people being able to afford to fly is a good thing, and flying is still safer than driving. The failure is an FAA air traffic organization system that hasn’t kept up with demand. The Times has done excellent reporting on this subject. In August 2023, I covered its investigation into 46 close calls in one month and roughly 300 near collisions in a year. Staffing shortages, fatigue, outdated equipment and a culture that tolerated recurring warnings weren’t the result of the 2024 election. Fallows discusses 132 union-represented FAA employees fired during the DOGE purge. Their union announced their reinstatement in March 2025. Firing them was disruptive, but he leaves out the reversal. And recruiting gamers doesn’t establish that the FAA is lowering controller standards. The FAA has been growing the top of the funnel but there’s no indication that unqualified people are being advanced in training to a greater degree. I’ve criticized Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy for counting initial trainee hires as progress, that strikes me as juking the stats but hasn’t been a safety issue. And the Trump administration is doing modernization work that’s valuable and that’s been ignored for decades even if I’m skeptical that it’s close to enough because they don’t seem to be addressing the management problems at the FAA that created failed technology investment and decades-long controller shortages in the first place. I’ve been critical of pouring money into the existing organization. We need to address how air traffic control is managed, how it buys equipment, how it finances long projects and how it’s held accountable for performance. We’d be far better off separating the air traffic control operator from its regulator, with stable funding and professional management, as Canada has done with its nonprofit provider. The statement also thanks the administration for supporting the agency and providing resources. That last part is political messaging too. (Gratitude for a budget doesn’t answer address aviation safety, but is probably necessary as a defensive measure.)

“We are disappointed that this article sensationalizes and politicizes tragedies.” The NTSB’s response to the New York Times Magazine. — NTSB Newsroom (@NTSB_Newsroom) November 12, 2026

Fallows interviewed NTSB Chair Jennifer Homendy. The agency argues she’s being mischaracterized, but they don’t actually say how. In the article she says, “Recommendations alone don’t save lives. Action does.” Her own July statement about the Washington crash warned that delays implementing the board’s recommendations represented a continuing, unacceptable risk.

Fallows spends considerable space describing the 2009 Colgan Air crash near Buffalo – as the last U.S. airline crash with loss of life before American 5342. In fact, PenAir flight 3296 overran the runway at Unalaska in October 2019, killing a passenger. His account has the Colgan Air plane accumulating ice until it becomes a “frigid brick.” He writes that by the time the captain recognized the problem, it “might already have become unflyable.” But that isn’t what the investigation found. The FAA’s accident analysis says icing wasn’t a cause. When the stall warning activated, the airplane wasn’t yet stalled. The captain pulled back on the controls and put it into a stall. The crew had failed to monitor their airspeed and mishandled the approach. And both Colgan pilots already had more than 1,500 hours: The captain had 3,379 hours. The captain had a history of training failures despite the hours. The first officer had 2,244 hours, including 1,470 hours as a light airplane flight instructor and 774 hours in turbine aircraft. The 1,500 Hour Rule For Co-Pilots Isn’t The Reason For The Safety Improvements After Colgan, Congress and regulators made several changes. Pilot rest rules improved. Training changed. Pilot qualification requirements changed. Sharing pilot records improved. Evidence that safety has performed well after a package of changes doesn’t establish that (1) every element of those changes (2) caused the improvements. And there’s strong reason to believe the the 1,500 hour rule has the opposite effect. It makes becoming a pilot take far longer and become far more costly, which keeps talented people from becoming pilots. Those with a higher opportunity cost, or lacking access to resources needed, get excluded from the pool of potential pilots. You’re then drawing from a smaller group. The NTSB didn’t recommend the 1,500 hour number. In 2010 Senate testimony, then-chair Deborah Hersman explained that the board had investigated accidents involving both high-time and low-time pilots. It found a relationship between failed practical tests and subsequent accidents, but there wasn’t evidence that number of hours actually mattered. The higher minimum took effect in 2013. Fallows’s safety streak begins in February 2009. The first four and a half years of that streak can’t be credited to a requirement that wasn’t yet in effect. Making pilots chase hours can allow relevant skills to deteriorate and bad habits to develop before an airline has to train those habits out of them. Repeated flights in clear weather, making takeoffs and landings at the same familiar airports, aren’t equivalent to structured training for instrument flying in a jet with another pilot, airline procedures, and complex air traffic control instructions. Hours in a hot air balloon count. The hot air balloon can be tethered. European co-pilots don’t face the same restriction, but their safety record is equivalent. Sub-1,500 hour European pilots fly in the United States every day without concern (and American pilots fly in Europe without elevated risk). The argument for the rule is that experience can build judgment, and that the higher threshold was part of a successful improvement in regional airline standards. But that’s an argument for useful experience and rigorous standards. That’s not what we got. An hour spent building time isn’t as valuable as an hour spent in a supervised airline training program. And there’s zero evidence that 1,500 is the right metric. It was chosen because it’s expensive and time-consuming, restricts entry into the pilot profession and drives up wages, and was supported by the major pilots union. But its own probable cause finding identifies the helicopter crew’s failure to maintain effective visual separation as a cause of the collision. The crew accepted responsibility for avoiding the airliner and failed to do so. The existence of systemic failures don’t eliminate pilot error. In Homendy’s testimony explaining the findings, the board concluded that the helicopter’s altimeter probably understated its altitude by about 100 feet. The crew likely believed it was below the route’s ceiling. Fallows also describes the crew flying through civilian traffic without monitoring its traffic avoidance systems. The helicopter had no integrated cockpit traffic display. The pilots had tablets capable of showing traffic, but looking down at them while flying low at night with night vision goggles was problematic. The Times has done excellent reporting on this subject. In August 2023, I covered its investigation into 46 close calls in one month and roughly 300 near collisions in a year. The Times has done excellent reporting on this subject. In August 2023, I covered its investigation into 46 close calls in one month and roughly 300 near collisions in a year. Staffing shortages, fatigue, outdated equipment and a culture that tolerated recurring warnings weren’t the result of the 2024 election. Fallows discusses 132 union-represented FAA employees fired during the DOGE purge. Their union announced their reinstatement in March 2025. Firing them was disruptive, but he leaves out the reversal. And recruiting gamers doesn’t establish that the FAA is lowering controller standards. The FAA has been growing the top of the funnel but there’s no indication that unqualified people are being advanced in training to a greater degree. I’ve criticized Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy for counting initial trainee hires as progress, that strikes me as juking the stats but hasn’t been a safety issue. And the Trump administration is doing modernization work that’s valuable and that’s been ignored for decades even if I’m skeptical that it’s close to enough because they don’t seem to be addressing the management problems at the FAA that created failed technology investment and decades-long controller shortages in the first place. I’ve been critical of pouring money into the existing organization. We need to address how air traffic control is managed, how it buys equipment, how it finances long projects and how it’s held accountable for performance. We’d be far better off separating the air traffic control operator from its regulator, with stable funding and professional management, as Canada has done with its nonprofit provider. Fallows combines airline accidents, rocket debris, military encounters, airshow incidents and presidential flyovers which generally have different causes. And a list that attributes incidents while a given President is in office to that President doesn’t do a good job of evaluating what decisions caused those incidents. But more simply, even the article’s own figures for the most serious runway incursions are problematic for the Fallows narrative:

The increase from 2024 to 2025 looks dramatic – a doubling! But it’s still far lower than under Biden in 2023! These are relatively small numbers, and they don’t show a steadily worsening trend. (And there are more aircraft operations than in 2023.)

Fallows points to deregulation and falling fares as sources of overcrowding. More people being able to afford to fly is a good thing, and flying is still safer than driving. The failure is an FAA air traffic organization system that hasn’t kept up with demand.

The Times has done excellent reporting on this subject. In August 2023, I covered its investigation into 46 close calls in one month and roughly 300 near collisions in a year. Staffing shortages, fatigue, outdated equipment and a culture that tolerated recurring warnings weren’t the result of the 2024 election.

Fallows discusses 132 union-represented FAA employees fired during the DOGE purge. Their union announced their reinstatement in March 2025. Firing them was disruptive, but he leaves out the reversal.

And recruiting gamers doesn’t establish that the FAA is lowering controller standards. The FAA has been growing the top of the funnel but there’s no indication that unqualified people are being advanced in training to a greater degree. I’ve criticized Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy for counting initial trainee hires as progress, that strikes me as juking the stats but hasn’t been a safety issue.

And the Trump administration is doing modernization work that’s valuable and that’s been ignored for decades even if I’m skeptical that it’s close to enough because they don’t seem to be addressing the management problems at the FAA that created failed technology investment and decades-long controller shortages in the first place.

I’ve been critical of pouring money into the existing organization. We need to address how air traffic control is managed, how it buys equipment, how it finances long projects and how it’s held accountable for performance. We’d be far better off separating the air traffic control operator from its regulator, with stable funding and professional management, as Canada has done with its nonprofit provider.

‘Ramayana,’ ‘Fatherland,’ ‘Dhurandhar’ Lead International Film Festival of India Lineup Featuring Almodovar, Kore-eda, Kurosawa

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Paweł Pawlikowski’s “Fatherland,” which earned him the best director prize at Cannes this year, will open the 57th International Film Festival of India (IFFI), set for Nov. 20-28 in Goa.

Nitesh Tiwari’s “Ramayana: Rise of a Legend” will open the festival’s BRICS showcase, and Aditya Dhar’s Bollywood feature “Dhurandhar” will open the Indian Panorama.

Goa chief minister Pramod Sawant unveiled the program at a curtain-raiser press conference in the state, joined by festival director Ashutosh Gowariker, the filmmaker behind “Lagaan.” Organizers received more than 1,900 submissions from 137 countries for this edition, and the lineup spans more than 150 international films from 86 countries. Related Stories Gkids Acquires U.S. Rights to Cartoon Saloon’s Animated Film 'Julián,' Directed by Louise Bagnall and Executive Produced by Zoe Saldaña (EXCLUSIVE) Arnold Schwarzenegger Explains to Jimmy Kimmel Why He’s ‘Absolutely’ Still a Republican After the Blue ‘Tsunami’ Midterms

“Fatherland” is set in 1949. It tracks Nobel laureate Thomas Mann and his daughter Erika as they travel through a Germany split by the aftermath of war. The Poland, Germany, Italy and France co-production plays in German, English, French and Russian.

Alina Șerban’s “I Matter,” a Romania, Germany and Belgium co-production in Romanian and Romani, will screen as the mid-fest film. Anthony Chen’s Singaporean comedy-drama “We Are All Strangers,” in Mandarin and English, will close the festival.

Chanchal Kumar, secretary of India’s Ministry of Information & Broadcasting, quoted Prime Minister Narendra Modi in discussing this year’s theme, “Stories Without Borders”: “The stories may have an address, but emotions have no borders.”

The BRICS section will feature work from all 11 member nations of the bloc as well as its partner countries. Titles include Jessica Beshir’s “Faya Dayi” and Anastasia Kuzina’s “Bo Bo.”

Spain will bring four films as country of honor: Pedro Almodóvar’s “Bitter Christmas,” Jaime Lorente’s “The Bad Son,” Laura Alvea and José Ortuño’s “Trinidad” and Juana Macías’ “Looking for Michael.” Russia’s partner-country showcase pairs Kuzina’s “Bo Bo” with Nikita Grushin’s “New Home,” Raul Geidarov’s “Sidney Lumet’s Birthday” and Gerbert Rappaport’s musical “Cheryomushki.”

The festival will screen 76 Indian films across 18 languages. Indian Panorama selectors weighed 404 feature and 358 non-feature submissions, while the best web series (OTT) category attracted 46 eligible entries. Leena Yadav’s Ladakhi-language “Becoming” will open the non-feature strand.

Madhur Bhandarkar chairs the feature film jury and Himanshu Shekhar Khatua heads the non-feature film jury.

Indian director-producer Aanand L. Rai (“Raanjhanaa,” “Tanu Weds Manu”) will chair the international competition jury. He will be joined by Argentine filmmaker Sol Berruezo Pichon-Riviere; Thai writer-director Ratchapoom Boonbunchachoke, whose “A Useful Ghost” won the grand prize at Cannes Critics’ Week in 2025; Colombian sound designer María Alejandra Rojas; and Romanian writer-director Bogdan Mureșanu (“The New Year That Never Came”).

The Cinema of the World section includes Kore-eda Hirokazu’s “Look Back,” Kurosawa Kiyoshi’s “The Samurai and the Prisoner,” Lukas Dhont’s “Coward,” Nishikawa Miwa’s “Children Untold,” Gábor Reisz’s “Ground Floor” and Wim Wenders’ “From Inside Out – The Architecture of Peter Zumthor.” From Festivals Worldwide gathers Hong Sang-soo’s “Nowhere to Lay My Eyes,” Emin Alper’s “Salvation,” Valeska Grisebach’s “The Dreamed Adventure” and Grant Gee’s “Everybody Digs Bill Evans.”

Two sections are new this year, with five films each. Animation includes Brad Bird’s “Ray Gunn.” Films About Films was created after leading filmmakers suggested it. Its titles include Barnaby Thompson’s “Maverick: The Epic Adventures of David Lean” and Francesco Zippel’s “Vittorio De Sica – Staging Life.”

Seven first features will compete for the best debut feature prize: “Fading Man” (Germany), “The Sledge” (India), “Paris” (Belgium), “The Black Sea” (Egypt), “L’Âge d’Or (The Golden Age)” (France and Italy), “Birds on Ice” (India) and “Atlantic” (China). The wider program counts 52 women directors and 62 first-time filmmakers.

Restored Classics will screen Don Siegel’s “Invasion of the Body Snatchers,” Danny Boyle’s “Trainspotting,” David Lynch’s “Wild at Heart,” F.W. Murnau’s “Sunrise: A Song of Two Humans” and Ernst Lubitsch’s “To Be or Not to Be.” Andrzej Wajda gets a five-film centenary tribute. Indian figures born a century ago will also be honored: directors Shakti Samanta and Raj Khosla, actors Uttam Kumar, Suryakant Mandhare, Prem Nazir and Raj Kumar, and composers O.P. Nayyar and Ravi.

The WAVES Film Bazaar market will host 150 invited guests, including buyers and production companies. Its program covers a Screenwriters’ Lab, a Co-production Market, a Viewing Room and a Work-in-Progress Lab. Netflix is the bazaar’s Knowledge Series partner and will present the gala premiere of “Pushpak Viman,” alongside an international screening of “Ray Gunn.” The streamer will also highlight its work with the Indian government on building the country’s VFX talent base.

Screenings will take place on 13 indoor screens at four locations: Panaji, Porvorim, Margao and Ponda. Together they seat more than 4,000.

IFFI was launched in 1952 and ranks among Asia’s oldest film festivals. It is organized by the information and broadcasting ministry with the Goa government and the Entertainment Society of Goa.

4 Hours Saved, 20 Hours In The Air: Will Passengers Choose Qantas’ Project Sunrise?

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Well over 20 hours on a single flight sounds like hell to a lot of passengers. But enduring this will be necessary for those wishing to use Qantas Project Sunrise flights, which are set to begin in 2027 with services from Sydney Kingsford-Smith Airport (SYD) to London Heathrow Airport (LHR).

Qantas will also demand a premium, arguing that passengers can expect to save around four hours and avoid the hassle of an international transfer. Despite the clear difficulties that Project Sunrise could pose, particularly for economy class passengers, Qantas is banking on its success.

Airways Magazine reports that the airline expects to add AU$400 million ($274 million) in earnings before interest and tax through the services. High demand will be vital to secure such positive results, particularly given that challenges such as high fuel costs, a weakened Australian dollar and higher labor and airport costs have emerged since 2022.

Project Sunrise's opening route will be between SYD and LHR, a mammoth route of approximately 9,188 nautical miles (17,000 km). Traveling from Britain to Australia currently usually requires a change, which is often in Singapore or the Middle East. Such stopovers make the route longer, and passengers also have to waste time on the ground.

It is expected that Project Sunrise flights will take around 21 hours and 40 minutes. This will save approximately four hours. In return for the time savings and the unique nature of this flight, Qantas expects to receive a 20% higher Revenue per Available Seat Kilometer (RASK) compared with comparable one-stop itineraries.

When it arrives in 2027, Project Sunrise will overtake Singapore Airlines Airbus A350-900ULR flights as the world's longest. That will secure Qantas' position as the leading premium airline in Australia and a key player among the airlines that offer ultra-long-haul flying. However, regardless of the added convenience, some passengers will struggle with being on an aircraft for over 20 hours, while others will find the higher price off-putting.

41% of seats will be in premium cabins, versus 30% in premium cabins for Qantas' Boeing 787-9 and Airbus A380, Airways Mag reports. These seats are divided between first, business, and premium economy class. Such a high number of premium seats takes inspiration from Singapore Airlines' A350-900ULRs, which are exclusively fitted with premium economy and business class seats.

It's not surprising that Qantas expects these products to be popular, given that a flight of over 20 hours will give passengers the opportunity to take advantage of the luxury. The table below uses Qantas' data to show the key specifications for the A350-1000ULR. It covers both the aircraft itself and the four cabins onboard.

9,700-10,000 nautical miles (18,000-18,520 km)

The ultimate in luxury comes from first class, a product that Singapore Airlines has skipped on its A350-900ULR. These six suites are distinguished from business class by a separate armchair and a true flat bed, rather than one that reclines. This class also has a huge entertainment screen, along with a flexible space for eating and working with up to two guests.

Behind first class is an enormous 52-seat business class cabin. The suite has a privacy door and totals 42 inches (100 cm) wide. At 80 inches (2 m), the flat bed is one inch (2.5 cm) longer than what Qantas offers on its Airbus A380 business class product. All but the very tallest of guests should find this roomy enough. Finally, customers can choose from within a 40-seat premium economy cabin.

These seats are in a 2-4-2 configuration and have 40 inches (100 cm) of pitch. Australian designer David Caon says that “the Premium Economy cabin has been redesigned from the ground up with a focus on ergonomics, entertainment and privacy.” The new headrest wings are the biggest visible difference and will provide passengers with both additional support and a sense of privacy without isolating them from travel companions.

The main reason that Qantas could fail is an inability to sell economy class seats. Many passengers on a budget would rather take the hit of a longer total travel time for the chance to stretch their legs during a one-stop itinerary. Before the ongoing crisis in the Middle East, it was also popular to use the stop as an excuse to enjoy a short vacation in the region, such as in Dubai, before the main trip to Australia or the United Kingdom.

Qantas hopes it can sway wary passengers through a high-quality economy class product. The cabin has 140 seats, with 42 in Economy Plus. These seats are in a 3-3-3 configuration. A relatively generous 33 inches (83.8 cm) of pitch was initially promised, although Qantas has now backtracked to saying the pitch will vary in different parts of the aircraft.

22 inches (56 cm) in the separate reclining armchair

A six-way adjustable headrest gives a greater degree of customization compared to what you can usually expect in economy class. Two fast-charging outlets per seat also means that your entertainment won't run out. Economy Plus will be broadly the same but with priority boarding and access to overhead lockers, as well as a little bit more legroom. The table above uses Qantas' data to compare the seat dimensions that you can enjoy, or suffer in, across each class.

However, initial predictions are that Qantas will attract strong demand for its economy class seats aboard Project Sunrise, partly because it has intentionally produced a small economy class cabin. Executive Traveller's case study predicts a load factor of 85%. This is lower than what is expected in the premium classes, but still more than enough to sustain the service.

Regardless of the quality of the economy class, you would still be spending over 20 hours in a seat designed for affordability rather than comfort. Some airlines that fly ultra-long-haul routes are getting creative, such as United Airlines' Relax Row, which makes lying flat in economy class possible.

The Australian carrier's answer to make such a long time in economy class possible is its Wellbeing Zone, which is available to both economy and premium economy class passengers. Qantas calls it "an on board destination to promote movement, stretching and hydration throughout your flight."

Within the Wellbeing Zone, you'll find a selection of beverages and refreshments available through self-service. The area will also have monitors to guide you through movements, ensuring you can stay mobile during the flight. "Sculpted surfaces and integrated handles" will also facilitate stretching. Qantas Group Chief Executive Officer Alan Joyce said that:

“Fewer seats translate to more space for each customer and a dedicated Wellbeing Zone for travellers to stretch, help themselves to a snack and spend time out of their seat. We are the only airline in the world that will have a bespoke designed onboard stretch and movement space,".

Airbus' A350-1000ULR begins testing as Project Sunrise moves closer to reality.

Qantas has made a vast investment to order 12 bespoke A350-1000ULR aircraft that will carry out the services. However, it is also investing time and personnel to get Project Sunrise off the ground. It will train over 360 pilots and 1,200 cabin crew, or 30 pilots and 100 cabin crew per aircraft, in the unique skills required for these services.

Such a substantial training operation is needed because of regulatory arrangements Qantas has made with the Civil Aviation Safety Authority, requiring far more crew members aboard every Project Sunrise flight. The Project Sunrise pilot training is occurring at a simulator in Sydney.

It is Australia's first A350 simulator, which was installed ahead of the first A350-1000ULR's arrival. The first cohort has been drawn from Qantas' current A330 pilots, with 40 involved in the first wave. That decision was made because of numerous commonalities between the A350 and the A330.

It's a race against time to get the crews ready for the expected first A350-1000ULR delivery in April 2027. It is currently expected that this first aircraft will fly to New Zealand to take training out of the simulator and familiarize the crew with its operations. The jet will then be pressed into regular service to the United Kingdom and, later, the US.

Jamie Dornan & Anthony Mackie Share Hilarious Lesson From Fatherhood

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Jamie Dornan and Anthony Mackie—who are costarring in the thriller series 12 12 12—exclusively told E! News about their kids' reactions to growing up with movie star dads.

Jamie Dornan and Anthony Mackie's most important critics are their kids.

The actors—who first starred in 2019's Synchronic before reuniting for upcoming TV series 12 12 12—confessed that amid their many roles as action stars over the years, earning cool points from their children has been no easy feat.

"If they don't think I'm cool after Lord of the Rings, I'm done," Dornan, who plays Aragorn in the upcoming prequel, The Hunt for Gollum, joked in an exclusive interview with E! News’ Will Marfuggi. "If that's not good enough for my kids, then I don't know what else to offer them."

The 50 Shades of Grey alum brought his three kids Dulcie, 13, Elva, 10 and Alberta, 7, with wife Amelia Warner to stay with him in New Zealand during the fantasy shoot, where they were thankfully impressed by his performance on set.

"Usually, they ask to leave. Like, they come to set, and after half an hour, they're like, 'You're just saying the same thing over and over. This is so boring,'" Dornan admitted. "They came to set one night when I was fighting and beheading orcs and stuff, and they were like, 'It was cool.'"

That said, the 44-year-old was pleased that his little ones found it "good to see Daddy all heroic like that" because, as he put it, "that'll be as cool as it gets for me."

Mackie's brood, on the other hand—which includes four sons with ex-wife Sheletta Chapital—weren't so easily won over by their dad moonlighting as a soldier with supernatural powers, but for good reason. The Captain America actor is already a superhero at home.

"My boys could care less," Mackie remarked with a laugh. "At the end of the day, I realized they just want me to be dad."

And the 48-year-old understands why his family unit is more content with quality time at home, including playing pickleball.

"I love the fact that all they want from me is me," he continued. "But then when I come home, we get to be one big family."

Still, Mackie admitted bringing the crew as his plus-fours to a red carpet was more than a typical 'bring your kids to work' day, even for them.

"They went to the premiere of my movie," Mackie recounted. "They had never seen anything of that scale, like of that magnitude."

And Mackie and Dornan's latest project is yet another grand production. The Marvel alum credited the art department when remarking, "The scale of the show, I feel, is kind of like another character."

The Belfast native agreed the ambitious approach paid off for the series, premiering Nov. 13 on Apple TV, about a meticulously planned fictional robbery of a bank in Zurich.

"It's like the biggest heist in European history," Dornan said, using the British slang for cheap when adding, "It couldn't be pony. It needed to be big."

New episodes drop on Fridays through Dec. 25.

Man City have 'certain' Gianluigi Donnarumma exit route, hurdle must be overcome - Expert

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Manchester City could potentially offload struggling goalkeeper Gianluigi Donnarumma to the Saudi Pro League next summer.

That is according to former Everton chief executive Keith Wyness, speaking exclusively to Football Insider, who believes that the Citizens' options are limited in terms of where they can move Donnarumma.

The 27-year-old has faced scrutiny for his performances of late, with his current 65.8 per cent save percentage standing him 13th in the ranking of Premier League starting goalkeepers.

Wyness believes that the Saudi Pro League could provide a genuine option for City to cash in on the Italian, whose wages are likely to pose an issue for several European clubs.

Donnarumma was widely regarded as one of the world's leading shot-stoppers when he arrived at the Etihad Stadium back in 2025, but is seeing his reputation damaged a little of late.

The former PSG ace is on a significant contract with the Citizens, but is not producing the goods for them at the moment.

Everton’s former chief Wyness – who served as CEO at Goodison Park between 2004 and 2009 and now runs a football consultancy advising elite clubs – believes that a move to Saudi Arabia could be an option for Donnarumma, but is clear that City would have to play the market carefully.

The Pro League recently introduced a new budget cap which prevents clubs from spending over 75 per cent of their revenue on their first-team squad.

Speaking on the latest edition of Football Insider's Inside Track podcast, Wyness explained how that has created a hurdle for City to overcome with Donnarumma.

"Yeah, I do (think he could be sold). I mean, if this would have been just a year ago, I'd have said the Saudi market would probably have taken him up," he said.

"But, you know, the budget restrictions may be starting to bite down there slightly. So it's not quite the option for clubs that it once was.

"Although, in Donnarumma's case, I still think it could be one option, certainly."

Former Man City financial advisor Stefan Borson recently told Football Insider that Donnarumma is becoming a "very expensive problem" for his former club.

The football finance expert believes that Enzo Maresca and his staff simply cannot continue to accept the gloveman's mistakes longt-

Borson believes that a return to Italy is the clear leading option for Donnarumma in terms of an escape route, but pointed out that he would likely need to take a sizeable pay cut.

The shot-stopper is becoming a big issue for the Citizens, especially as their 114 financial charges continue to loom over them.

#PostPulpit mailbag: What’s going on with Christian Gonzalez?

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The New England Patriots have landed in Germany where they hold a pair of practices before taking on the Detroit Lions at 9:30 a.m. ET on Sunday.

So, let's get into this week's edition of the #PostPulpit Mailbag.

Like a lot of people, I was mightily disappointed (to say the least) in the recent performance of our resident highest-paid cornerback in the league. But I find myself wondering if he's the only one deserving blame. When he repeatedly lines up in such soft coverage, not pressing at the line of scrimmage — indeed, giving the receiver an obscenely huge cushion to release — that's not entirely HIS decision, right? Isn't that pre-snap alignment dictated by the coaches and their game plan, at least much of the time? How much of the problem do you think lies with coaching schemes that play away from Gonzalez's strengths? – BriGuy301

What happened to Gonzalez last game and why? Was it a one-off, or did the Packers figure something out that other teams might be able to emulate? – fdsafdsa

Gonzalez is certainly not blameless and needs to play better. However, he is also being asked to do a lot within a Patriots secondary that leans heavily on disguising coverages. While the All-Pro cornerback is at his best pressing receivers at the line of scrimmage and playing man coverage, he has been asked to handle a variety of responsibilities, including off-man coverage, inverted Cover 2 (where he is responsible for the half-field safety), playing the deep third in Cover 3 and more.

The question here is whether the Patriots have leaned too heavily into those disguises and reached a point where simplifying things would benefit the defense. New England has a talented secondary that is playing well below its capabilities as they rank dead last in the league in zone coverage EPA per dropback. Opposing offenses like the Bears and Packers have also appeared to be one step ahead with coverage beaters and using tools like quick snaps to catch the defense out of position and generate explosive plays.

At some point, the Patriots may need to prioritize letting their defensive backs play more straight up and to their strengths.

With all the pressure that Maye faces each game, and especially against teams like the Packers who blitz a lot (46.5% of his drop backs against Green Bay), why do we not see more hot reads or short patterns? It looks like the Patriots rely predominantly on mid- to long-range routes, which take more time and require the Offensive Line to hold up longer. I would think that Pop or Chism – who both have short-area quickness – would be great at those routes, especially given that they seem to run a lot of them during the preseason. So I'm curious if A) they're doing it but I just don't see it? And/or B) is there a good reason to not run quick slants and crossers and hot reads? – PatsFanFromNH

The Patriots' offense does feature sight adjustments and hot routes. Earlier this season, players credited opposing defenses for dropping defenders off the line of scrimmage to take away those routes. New England will need to find ways to counter those adjustments or develop other answers against the pressure packages that have repeatedly given the offense trouble.

In recent weeks, both the Bears and Packers have found success attacking the Patriots' offensive line with overload pressures, sending two defenders off the same side of the formation and dropping defenders off the line of scrimmage on the other side. In both games, New England struggled to pick up those pressures early before settling down and finding answers as the game progressed.

Moving forward, the Patriots need to determine how they want to handle those situations out of the gate, whether that means identifying the right protection calls before the snap or trusting Maye and his receivers to make plays after. Finding those answers earlier in games will be critical to avoid early deficits.

Brian, if the Patriots lose to the Lions to fall to 5-5 with 4 very difficult games left, is it time to switch gears and root for draft position? It's been a tough year full of injuries. That happens in football, and the draft is supposed to be a good one. On the other hand, if they pull off the upset in Germany, it's full speed ahead going for the playoffs, with a bye week plus the Chargers game to get healthy. – New England Guy

Sunday's game against the Lions feels like an inflection point on the season, so it will be interesting to see which Patriots team shows up in Germany.

As for the playoffs, 10 wins has historically been a good benchmark for securing a postseason spot. If the Patriots take care of business against the winless Chargers, Jets and Dolphins, they would need just two more wins from their remaining matchups against the Lions, Vikings, Chiefs, Bills and Broncos to reach that mark.

And looking at the current AFC landscape, the threshold could be closer to nine wins. There appears to be a relatively clear-cut top seven in the conference unless the Houston Texans catch fire during the second half of the season.

Why didn't the pats trade for a nose tackle, 3rd safety, or/and a 3rd outside cornerback? Any one, two or all 3 of these positions were a must have to right the ship! – sjamesbossports

After investing significant draft capital in A.J. Brown and looking to avoid taking on a major financial commitment, the Patriots were never likely to make a significant addition at the trade deadline. You could certainly make the argument for adding defensive depth in exchange for late-round draft capital, but the lack of movement at those positions around the league suggests there wasn't much of a market at those spots.

A question more long term :what are your thoughts about next year patriots cap big moves renegotiation with major players (or potential cut or trade) to absorb anticipated Maye super contract ? – 7i165

Who do you think will be the cap casualties next year? Onwenu, Barmore, Davis, Hunter, Byard, DeVitto, Moses, Landry? I know they are a veteran core, but almost all of them don't produce to what they are being paid at, so we might need to cut bait. How much would we gain from trading or letting them walk? – Maxxwell

The Patriots did not sell at the deadline as they still can be a competitve team especially as they get healthier on offense. It will be a different conversation this offseason as there is definitely an argument for the team getting younger and adding draft capital.

Among the names mentioned, I would imagine the team moves on from Christian Barmore, Harold Landry, and potentially Carlton Davis as the veterans have no guaranteed money remaining. Byard's play has not been worthy of a second contract while the guess would be Moses retires. Henry was extended this offseason while there's no rush to move off DeVito. All the moves would free up a substantial amount of salary cap space.

That's all for this week's #PostPulpit mailbag. If you have questions you'd liked to be answered next week, submit them online in our weekly submission post on the Feed. Make sure to follow @iambrianhines and @PatsPulpit as well.
